AI and Inequality: Nobel-Winning Economist Daron Acemoglu on How to Get AI Right

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·39:52·3.5K views·2026-10-29
Daron AcemogluEconomist
TL;DRLiberal democracy faces a 50-year crisis driven by rising inequality and social silos, but Daron Acemoglu argues that steering AI away from pure automation and toward "pro-worker AI" can restore shared prosperity and human agency.
Historical Achievement: Liberal democracy successfully combined individual freedoms with state-provided opportunities, infrastructure, and insurance to create shared prosperity. Transition to Post-Industrial Society: Over the last 50 years, globalization and digital...

TL;DR

Liberal democracy faces a 50-year crisis driven by rising inequality and social silos, but Daron Acemoglu argues that steering AI away from pure automation and toward "pro-worker AI" can restore shared prosperity and human agency.

The Crisis of Liberal Democracy and Its 50-Year Roots

  • Historical Achievement: Liberal democracy successfully combined individual freedoms with state-provided opportunities, infrastructure, and insurance to create shared prosperity.
  • Transition to Post-Industrial Society: Over the last 50 years, globalization and digital technologies fundamentally altered the economy, shifting economic power away from workers and severing the link between business expansion and worker wage growth.
  • Geographic and Social Silos: College-educated professionals in urban hubs like New York, Boston, and London became physically and socially segregated from non-college workers, eroding empathy and creating a deep political divide.
  • Political Realignment: Working-class voters who historically supported center-left parties (like the U.S. Democrats or British Labour) have shifted toward center-right or hard-right movements due to perceived establishment neglect.

The Threat of Misdirected Artificial Intelligence

  • The AGI and Superintelligence Trap: Spending over $1 trillion primarily on foundational models and artificial general intelligence (AGI) threatens to automate vast portions of the workforce, making workers dispensable and vastly increasing inequality.
  • The Algorithmic Cage: Social media business models based on attention capture and targeted ads use algorithms to police and isolate users into extreme behavioral norms rather than fostering genuine deliberation and bridges.
  • False Dichotomies: Media narratives framing AI as either boundless prosperity or civilization-destroying killer robots obscure the real debate over the *direction* of technological development.

Pro-Worker AI and Working-Class Liberalism

  • Working-Class Liberalism: Acemoglu proposes a new governing philosophy focused on shared prosperity, quality jobs for non-college workers, and community self-government anchored in liberal individual rights.
  • Pro-Worker AI Definition: Utilizing AI as a versatile information technology that provides real-time, context-relevant data to expand human expertise and problem-solving rather than replacing workers.
  • Human-Complementary Examples:
    • Healthcare: Empowering nurses to take a greater role in diagnosis, care, and prescribing drugs.
    • Education: Enabling a single teacher to approach cost-effective individualized education programs.
    • Trades: Equipping electricians and plumbers to handle increasingly complex problems.
  • The Michigan vs. Germany Robot Comparison: When industrial robots were introduced, Detroit (Ford, GM, Chrysler) laid off workers, whereas Germany retrained operators and welders to become higher-skilled technicians, resulting in superior productivity and lower inequality.

Economic Levers, Business Models, and Policy Shifts

  • Tax Code Distortions: Current tax systems in advanced economies heavily penalize labor while subsidizing capital, sending a powerful signal to firms to automate.
  • Business Model Reprioritization: Business leaders should view workers as assets to make more productive rather than mere costs to cut by 2-5%.
  • Government Intervention: While governments cannot act as AI entrepreneurs, they can update tax policy, protect data privacy, and incentivize high-quality data investments for pro-worker models.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Probability of Achieving Pro-Worker AI and Liberal Renewal: Daron Acemoglu estimates the likelihood of society making the necessary investments in both liberal democracy and pro-worker AI at 30 to 40 percent.
  • Four-Day Workweek Assessment: Acemoglu views the 4-day workweek as a questionable solution if it is driven by the premise that jobs are permanently disappearing, though he is more sympathetic if it stems from workers gaining wealth and needing time for continuous learning and leisure.
  • AI Agents Prediction: Acemoglu predicts that humans will not universally end up working for AI agents, as AGI will not be achieved anytime soon and humans will continue to control AI systems.

Notable Quotes

"If we start sacrificing our institutions, we risk losing all of these things very badly." — Daron Acemoglu
"There's nothing in the nature of AI that it should do those things. It's how we develop and use these technologies." — Daron Acemoglu

He is going to explain the crisis as he sees it. The crisis that is widening the economic divide, what's needed to fix it, and also the role that AI, especially pro-worker AI, could play in solving this issue. Welcome to Daron Acemoglu. Thank you, Linda. It's my pleasure to be with you. We are very, very honored to have you. Why don't we get started, dig right in? What is this crisis? What is the biggest crisis that is facing the economies and humanity right now as you see it? I believe that liberal democracy is one of humanity's greatest achievements. If you look at our past, the million year evolution, the existence in fighting bands or very unfree societies, people at that point would have had a very hard time imagining that we could build a complex society with very different people but all cohabiting, participating in politics, mostly peacefully, and using state institutions for creating also shared prosperity, meaning economic growth from which many people, most demographic groups are also taking their fair share. That is just an incredible achievement, but the more immediate challenge is that I think that achievement is now in danger, and we need to understand why liberal democracy is in crisis today and how we can rebuild it because liberal ideas and liberal democratic institutions, I am convinced, have a special role to play in our future as well, and we can make them better so that they can play that role. And just to get our listeners and our watchers sort of all on the same page, can you define liberal democracy as you see it and as you wrote about it in the book? Liberalism at its heart is about individual freedoms, freedom of expression, association, religion, but also about turning those into an actual behavior in which people can realize their aspirations, and that requires state institutions that provide opportunities, education, infrastructure, insurance against very tough things happening to people such as disability or inability to earn a living from their families, but all the time consistent with liberal principles. And liberal democracy is the most successful institutionalization of those liberal ideas because it brings elements of liberal thought and principles together with democratic participation. If we're not able to kind of get liberal democracy out of crisis, what happens? Today, liberal democracy is viewed as the establishment and anti-establishment feelings are everywhere, and not only those movements that challenge liberal democracy are illiberal, they're not democratic, they don't want to provide voice and ability to self-government to people, but they are often ideologies that separate people, create more divisions, and also they are stances that have not been able to deliver any kind of welfare or improvement to people. So, we really would be looking at both bad ideas, sometimes hateful ideas, but would also have to put up with worse corruption, more arbitrary rule, no equality before the law, and ultimately nothing like shared prosperity which we are still aspiring for. The fault lines started around 50 years ago, you start to see them. Let's talk a little bit about that to kind of understand what's shaping this, sort of what's so really kind of putting shared prosperity into great risk. Can you take us through sort of what changed at the sort of the end of the industrial era to the post-industrial era. What was this change that we saw? I think if you want to understand what's going on with liberal democracy, you have to start with diagnosis, but I would say the roots of our crisis go back at least 50 years. Liberal democracy's problems are rooted in the transition from industrial society to a post-industrial society. By post-industrial, I mean an economy in which digital technologies play an important role, globalization combines supply chains and flow of goods much more seamlessly around the world. And these processes fundamentally change the nature of the economy, in particular making it much harder to reach shared prosperity. Not impossible, and there's nothing inevitable about either globalization or digital technologies, but the way that things have transpired in the United States and many other industrialized economies was that one of the major channels for creating shared prosperity which went from businesses innovating, expanding, producing more to workers also benefiting which was through businesses' desire to hire workers. And in a democratic framework with strong labor unions, that meant they had to pay higher wages as well as create jobs. And that was something that created a powerful drive towards shared prosperity because it meant that greater profits and greater revenues for businesses came hand in hand with higher wages. Digital technologies changed every aspect of that bargain. First of all, they started favoring more college-educated workers, who could take managerial positions, work with data, administrative positions, but something else more important was also at play. That was the use of digital technologies as a platform for automation. By automation, I mean the use of machinery and algorithms to replace tasks that were previously done by workers. And automation is a game changer because it severs the link between business expansion and shared prosperity. I want to be very clear that this is not in the nature of digital technologies that they always have to increase inequality. It's the way that we develop and use these technologies. Equally important, post-industrial society brought very different social relations. It elevated the social status and political power of college-educated workers, especially those integrated into the global economy in major urban centers such as in New York or Boston, but at the same time they started segregating into their own neighborhoods, their own social networks, their own communities, and diverging from the rest in terms of values. So, this created a tension in a broad coalition that was essential for liberal democracy. For example, you can see that in how center-left parties, the Labour Party in Britain, social democratic parties in Europe, or the Democratic Party in the United States, which used to receive the majority of their votes from working-class people, people without a college degree, working in manual or service jobs. Now, that demographic group votes for the center-right or the hard-right and center-left parties are overwhelmingly the party of choice for people who live in large urban areas with college degrees or postgraduate degrees. But center-left parties are essential for liberal democracy because they've been the pioneers of the welfare state, of shared prosperity, supporting the rights of workers, and now they became less concerned with the rise in inequality or the non-college groups in society falling behind and ultimately with the cultural politics of our era, even became a part of a big divide within the society that has created a major backlash. There's a lot of very unique examples you have in the book, in fact there's and you can correct me if I'm getting my if I'm remembering this wrong, with in Ohio, the Anchor Hocking company, where all of the people, the whether you worked in the factory, worked in the office, you were a manager, everybody's kids went to the same school, everybody lived on the same block, and then within a few decades, that was very much not the case, and certainly all of us sort of understand there's certain areas where elite people live and certain places where they don't. How important are those examples to sort of helping sort of drive home to people how these ideas walk and talk in their everyday? That you summarized it so well. Thank you for doing that, but they really tell the story. Not only has there been a big sea change that you don't see managers and workers living in the same community, sending their children to the same school, going to the same churches, but you have also broken one of the major ways in which you learn about the plight of other people or learn to empathize with them, and that has accelerated in the age of social media and online communication because we are interacting with each other much less in natural social networks. And that's very important for recognizing something that's really in hindsight is shocking in that 2016 election when Hillary Clinton was defeated by Donald Trump in the United States or in the 2016 Brexit referendum, most people who were very well-educated living in major cities like London, New York, Boston, were genuinely shocked that there was so much reaction against establishment. But that showed the extent of the discontent, but the shocking thing is how such strong feelings went unnoticed for almost three decades and that is because we came to live in silos. The major newspapers that we read are part of that silo. Who we talk to is part of that silo. Our education system is part of that silo, and that's not just a failure of our information ecosystem but it also makes it much harder for us to form bridges with each other to form that kind of broad consensus that are so important for democracy to work. People forget or overlook the connections between political institutions and economic prosperity. What is the before and after there? How does one feed into the other? Institutions are critical. Part of the reason why I am drawn to study and want to improve liberal democracy is because I believe liberal democratic institutions are essential for the kinds of lives that we aspire to, peaceful, based on dialogue and understanding, prosperous, and free. If we start sacrificing our institutions, we risk losing all of these things very badly. That's why the book talks both about liberal ideas, how they have evolved, how they worked, and why they were important for shared prosperity, voice, and other things. You write in the book, you say, "Liberalism's crisis is intimately linked to its failure to adapt to a changing world," and you argue that we should remake liberalism. Why is a new framework needed? First of all, clearly something's not working. So there is a real crisis here. People are willing to put the power ideas, leaders that really are very questionable in terms of their corruption, in terms of their incompetence, but it shows the strength of the feelings. I think liberalism needs a new governing philosophy. You cannot remake institutions without something that brings people together around their shared moral values and an understanding of how you could build a better society. That's what I mean by a governing philosophy. That is a collection of coherent ideas. Need to be part of that. In the past, for instance, social democratic ideas played a very important role in the development of the European welfare state, in the affirmation of the desirability of shared prosperity, of providing much higher quality public services to poorer parts of the countries. So we do need something like that again. Social democracy was for the industrial age, so that's why I emphasize something related but different. What I call working-class liberalism. And that is my rallying cry for this sort of new governing philosophy. AI could actually sort of enable this shift, right? You say, AI if steered correctly, can be complementary to the expansion of collective knowledge and the rebuilding of shared prosperity. And then you introduce this idea that we mentioned earlier in the introduction of pro-worker AI. Tell us what is pro-worker AI? Well, first, let me take a step back and again, I'm sort of partly responding to your previous question because I didn't want to go on and on, but working-class liberalism, at its heart has to be about shared prosperity, jobs, and better economic conditions for the working-class, people who don't have a college, who are going to work in manual jobs, service jobs, in a variety of trades. So that's essential. The other very big important element in working-class liberalism is community. So you have to bring communities in, give them room for self-government, to make different choices, experiment with their different values, and do that while being consistent with the founding principles of liberalism, individual freedom, individual rights to expression, and this drive for people to have voice. Now, taking it as face value, AI is a threat to both of them. If AI goes in the AGI, Artificial General Intelligence, and then artificial super intelligence direction, it could displace a very large fraction of the workforce from their jobs in the United States, in Europe, ultimately in the emerging world. That would make shared prosperity essentially impossible. It would make workers dispensable, not so important, second-class citizens so to speak. It would usher in an era of inequality far surpassing our very, very unequal present. But AI could also continue the trend of social media and pacify us more, take us away from our communities, don't encourage us to get into the messy but ultimately essential business of making compromises in real-world interactions, getting to understand different parties, different segments, perspectives, form bridges, deliberate with them. All of these things are a real threat to the future of liberal democracy, but here is the catch. There's nothing in the nature of AI that it should do those things. It's how we develop and use these technologies and the idea of pro-worker AI is about how we should conceptualize AI and how we could achieve it and why it might actually be complementary to building shared prosperity and making a better liberal democracy. And how does it walk and talk, pro-worker AI? My argument is that AI is not just a very powerful generative technology that could be used for automation or become the basis of something like artificial general or super intelligence, but it's also an amazingly versatile and powerful information technology, meaning that it can provide very relevant, accurate, context relevant information to individuals in real time. What that promises then is that all of us could become much better at solving problems in which we specialize, expanding our expertise, starting doing new things, new tasks, more innovation, provide better or different goods and services in our line of business, and those are very different from AGI or artificial super intelligence because they still have the worker in a critical part. We're making the worker's capabilities expand, you're making the worker perform new tasks. As a result, rather than being sidelined, this could increase demand for workers and re-energize that channel that I emphasize. Businesses doing well, but also paying higher wages and creating jobs, and that's so important for the future of shared prosperity and the future of liberal democracy. And what holds this back? Oh, that's not the direction in which we're going. Right now, there is more than a trillion dollar being spent on AI and a very large fraction of that goes into building more and more sophisticated foundation models. They're very, very impressive by the way. I will not sympathize with anybody who says these are just stochastic parrots. These are really capable models, and we can benefit a lot from them, but we also need to develop them in a way that could be complementary to humans. That's the pro-worker agenda. I give many examples in the book or in my other work, but there are so many places where you can see the promise of pro-worker AI. You can make nurses far better. They can play a much more important role in diagnosis, care, prescribing drugs, making follow-up decisions. You can make educators much more effective. Essentially, a single teacher can become with the help of AI much better at doing new things in terms of reorganizing the classroom and coming closer to a cost-effective but quite competent version of individualized education programs. You can make electricians or plumbers and all the trades much better because the problems that they are solving are becoming more complex. Now, you can give them tools that expand their expertise and enable them to do many more things than they could do. All of us working in knowledge occupations obviously can benefit from AI in the same way as well. All of us have heard the promise, like these wonderful things that could happen. But still, plenty of folks are equally worried, equally like gnawing on their fingernails. Why is it that this might not happen? Oh, they are absolutely 100%, 1000% right to be worried. This is not the direction we're going. And what holds it back? The kind of pro-worker AI I'm describing is actually not a big technological barrier. It is within our capabilities right now. There are a few companies that are working here, but they are marginal companies without the best scientists, the best model, the best GPUs, and they are making progress. So we need much more of the resources to go in this direction, and that's not what's happening. It's not what's happening because of the economic model of Silicon Valley, but it's also because of the ideology of Silicon Valley where, you know, all of the leaders are prioritizing building more and more human-like or human-improving models. You mention the algorithmic cage. Can you talk a little bit about that and how that impacts how we think and maybe also the direction we might go in? Communities, almost by their nature, create these cages of norms. They have their own norms, they try not to let people break out of them. When communities do that, we still have to protect individual liberty and that's the complex relationship between community and freedom that is at the heart of the book as well. What I call the algorithmic cage is that when the same thing is done, say by social media, you don't get many of the benefits, you get an even more powerful version of that cage of norms because now the algorithms are policing you or the algorithms are finding other people who are going to very strongly police you and are going to be very militant if necessary in forcing you into certain modes of behavior and it all of that happens without the shared moral values, the shared understanding, the development of information exchange etc that community brings. There's an interesting anecdote you have in the book where after the pandemic you went to a conference and you were all excited, you're like, "Oh gosh, you know this is a big deal. We're going to talk about wage stagnation and this is really important thing, people need to be digging in this, it has all these other implications," and then folks didn't talk about that. And you were like, "What?" What was going on? And why am I bringing that up? Because do you think that people are focused on the wrong signals? To some extent, yes, and that's again because of the algorithmic cage and this is because of the division between the college-educated groups that have become very dominant in politics throughout Europe and the United States, and the less-educated. So, wage inequality, you know, affects all of us but it's really the burden is on people without a college degree. In the United States, it's an extreme case, but it's a very telling extreme case. While wages of college graduate Americans have increased, those of postgraduate Americans have increased very rapidly. By the 35 years from 1980 onwards, the real inflation-adjusted earnings of non-college workers, non-college men, declined almost every year. So inequality discussions have to start there. That is just a phenomenally large change. But who are those bearing that burden? Those are the non-college workers, and if you start thinking that the non-college workers have the wrong values, they're not coming part of your coalition, you stop empathizing because you're not interacting with them as much, then that goes to the second rank of the problems that you focus on and that's exactly what I witnessed. You make the point in the book that business models can also influence political frameworks. Can you talk a little bit about that connection and how maybe that can help unlock better perspectives for AI? I think the most important aspect of the business model that we need to debate is what managers, CEOs view labor as. If you think that labor is a cost, that is going to push you more and more towards automation because if something is a cost, you just try to cut it. That's the right thing to do. But labor, your workers are also your main asset. And rather than just trying to limit the costs, what you should do is ask yourself, "How can I make that key asset be more productive?" That's where pro-worker AI would come. That's one aspect. Another aspect is the business model of, for example, social media. The social media mess that we have today, and I say very clearly in the book, social media is not responsible for the crisis of liberal democracy, but it is exacerbating it. But that mess is very related to the business model of the social media companies which is capture people's attention, and then make money by sending them individualized digital ads. That's a particular type of business model, but it's not consistent with the promise of digital technology and online technology that people believed in in the end of the 1990s or early 2000s, when, if you remember, everybody was talking about new economic democracy, news democracy, more organic forms of journalism, better communication and they were looking at what was happening in the Arab Spring and celebrating that as a gift of Facebook and Twitter. Well, these people weren't fully naive. Of course, their analysis turned out to be incorrect, but their aspirations weren't naive because digital technologies are fully capable of doing that. They can create new public spheres for us. They can provide us with better information and more effective ways of forming bridges and sharing information with each other, but it's just that social media's business model isn't that. If I'm a business leader listening to this and I read your book and there's that line where you say, you know, we have to get better at identifying pro-worker technologies, what do I do? What is my instinct that I'm moving towards and, and what's the thing I should be doing instead? Can you help them sort of right side? Business leaders who are actually producing things are very sympathetic. But what can they do? First of all, they need tools from the tech sector to actualize pro-worker AI and they're not getting that. Second, we've created expectations in the stock market through their boards that unless you go in lockstep in automating work using AI, you're falling behind your competitors. So they have to demonstrate to their boards and to their shareholders that they're doing that. So they're actually between a rock and a hard place as well. That's why the first step is changing the narrative. Davos has been at the heart of this agenda of automation, and businesses should make more profits, they should get leaner, we should build more complex networks of supply chains. I think Davos people should also ask themselves that question. Is this the right direction that we're pushing as the aspiration for our future? Nobody thinks that they're taking anything into a bad direction, right? Everybody is trying to do the right thing, I run the leadership podcast, everyone's a great listener, everyone's kind, but to help them have a gut check, is there a question that anyone listening to this or watching this can be asking themselves in different moments, different decision points to help gut check if they're going in the right direction? Think of two kinds of questions that should help. One is, if you actually start spelling out the kind of future we are going towards with more and more automation, I think ultimately you will realize that it's not a sustainable one. You cannot have democracy if a growing fraction of the population is poor, is dispensable, it feels and is viewed in society as not contributing anything to our collective good. I think that's a dystopian future. Second, if you think about your organization, what makes a real difference isn't whether you cut costs by two, or three, four, 5 percent. It's whether you do new things. The companies we remember from the past, such as Ford Motor Company, are not the ones that cut costs, they're the ones that revolutionized production by doing new things, and for that you need your most creative workers and you need to give them better tools. What are the misconceptions about the maybe the direction of jobs or how AI is impacting jobs? What's frustrating you about the conversation? I would say, mostly two things. The first one is the media's emphasis on this false dichotomy that either AI is going to bring boundless prosperity or it's going to create killer robots that destroy human civilization. The concerns that I'm expressing are somewhere in the middle. There will be advances, but we've pushed these advances in the wrong direction. The media and thought leaders have a role, making sure that the relevant kinds of information is provided to the public on this, and we've failed at that. Second, is the notion that somehow any democratic input into the direction of AI is stopping progress. So you hear that from many leaders in the tech sector or their supporters that if you want a better future, let these geniuses work. And that is the worst attitude you can have. AI is going to remake every aspect of our lives. And not just our lives, the lives of six billion people who are outside of the United States and China, whose lives are going to be shaped by AI as well. They have no voice and we have a voice. We live, we still live in a democracy. It is our responsibility to have a say about how our future is being remade, even if it's being remade by two or three companies. It doesn't mean that their CEOs have the right to make every decision. I think the democratic input is a very important part of that. If we get AI right, how does our everyday look different? In a way, in the same way that our everyday started looking different after electricity became part of our lives. Not everything went smoothly, but we started being much more productive, our factories became much more modern and effective, our lives became much better with household appliances that used electricity in the same way that our lives started becoming better after the internet, especially before the internet started going in our own direction, especially with social media, where we had access to much better services and information. With the early internet, we could do many things that we couldn't do before. There was more competition from companies to provide goods and services to us. And many, many, many new creative companies could be built via the internet. So AI is going to do many of those things if we play our cards right. And what's a tell it's going in the wrong direction? Once if we start seeing a particular statistic or a particular trend, what's something that should make us all sit up a little bit straighter? One telltale sign of automation is that labor's share of value added in industry or labor's share in national income is going down. We've been experiencing that for the last three decades, and if there is an acceleration in that, that's a sign that we are putting all of our eggs in the automation basket. There is nothing wrong with automation, by the way. Automation has been going on since 1750 and it's going to be part of our future as well. The problem is if we just do automation and we don't do the other things, creating that human complimentary AI, building on that pro-worker AI models. And that trend with that statistic, if it goes in the wrong direction, you know, why is that so important that we avoid that fate? Because it would usher in an era of unimaginable inequality and it would destroy our liberal democracy. What else? What about job destruction? Is there a job destruction, some kind of a statistic, some kind of a turning point we would get to where it's like, okay, gosh, now we, there's no turning back or we need to start kind of finding a new way? What are your thoughts about that as a tell? I don't think there is a job destruction threshold. Because if you actually look at the US economy, even when it was creating shared prosperity, it was destroying a lot of jobs and creating a lot of jobs. So I'm not saying that we should ultimately always have 3.5 million customer service representatives in the United States. Some of those jobs will go away. But here's the problem: if we destroy 3.5 million customer service representative jobs and we create no opportunities for these workers, that's a very different world than we actually give them opportunities to upskill and do even more meaningful and higher paying jobs. And you can see some of that contrast by comparing what happened when robots were introduced in Michigan versus in Germany. In Michigan, in Detroit, Flint, when robots were introduced by Ford, General Motors, Chrysler and their suppliers, they laid off workers. Operators, welders, painters, etc. In Germany, what they did is that they took those workers and they trained them to become technicians and higher skilled workers. And the implications are very different, both for productivity and for inequality. Is there a change that you would like to see happen in the year ahead in 2027? You like gosh, you know, if we can kind of make this steer, that'll be a step in the right direction. I think the ultimate huge success, which is impossible next year or even in a few years, is that the conversation changes such that a notable share of the amazing talent that's going into the tech sector right now goes there not to achieve AGI and make their science fiction dreams come to reality, but they go there because they think that AI can be developed in a pro-worker direction to improve our communication, to make our agency better, rather than worse. And what will it take to get there to make that change? I don't know. I wish I knew. It's not in here. It's not in there, but I'll tell you it's a social change, so it's a change of norms, first of all, but government policy can help. The government cannot steer AI without any constraints. The government cannot be the entrepreneur that does AI. But the government sets the framework. Taxes influence what happens. The United States and many other advanced economies have, in my opinion, a very distorted tax system that benefits capital, that benefits many people who are part of Davos because they make their money from capital, and punishes labor. Capital is taxed at such a lower rate. That's not just unfair and inequality inducing, it's actually bad for the future of AI because what it does is that it sends a signal to firms, a powerful signal, that they should automate because the government is subsidizing automation. So we have to change those tax policies. We also need support for more socially productive directions of AI. We need new laws so that people's data are protected and you encourage experts to invest in high-quality data that would be good for all kinds of AI models, but especially for pro-worker AI models. So there are many things that governments can do, but it's not going to be a silver bullet. I mean, I have a little rapid response section here to kind of close us out, where I'll give you some statement that we've all heard about sort of the inevitable direction for work and tech, and then you'll tell me whether you think it's going to happen or not. How about this one, one day we will all work for AI agents. I don't think so. AGI, I think, is not something we're going to be able to achieve any time soon, even though AI models are getting better and there will always be people who control AI as well. Not all of us will work for AI agents. We'll have a four-day workweek. Possible. I'm not sure. And, but I'm not also sure that that's the solution to our problems. How come? Because if you want to have a society in which there is wage growth and shared prosperity, you need to make labor more productive. Whereas the four-day drive comes from the idea that there's a fixed amount of jobs or a shrinking amount of jobs and we have to divide that better among the remaining humans and that's why we're going to make each human work less. It's very different from saying, you know, we're getting richer and richer, we have a lot to contribute to the production process, but we also have to do a lot of learning because we have longer lives and a more complex economy. Let's take that time for leisure or building our skills. That's very different. If that's the drive for a 4-day week, I'm much more sympathetic. Or if the drive for a 4-day week is that jobs are disappearing, there's nothing we can do about it, but throwing the towel against AI, let's cut it to 4 days or 3 days, that's a very different proposition and I'm not that much in support of that. Generalists, not specialists, will be the kings of the AI era. There could be some truth to that. If by generalist you mean people who have the flexibility to change what they do and holistically combine different perspectives, especially in a more innovative and creative way. We'll have pro-worker AI. I wish. I wish. I wish. How likely do you think it is? I am not giving up hope, but we do need a change of direction. Would you put a probability on it? I am very bullish that liberalism has the energy to recreate itself ultimately and I'm very bullish that if we put the effort to create pro-worker AI, we would do it with very good social outcomes as a consequence. Whether we'll make those investments in both liberal democracy and pro-worker AI, I would say that's certainly not more likely than 30 or 40 percent. So my optimism is about what we can achieve, not whether we'll actually get our act together and achieve it. And your last question, sir, what should leaders listening and watching this, what should they be prioritizing in the year ahead? I believe understanding AI is very, very important. But caring about liberal democracy's future, our ability to come together around shared moral values that bring us together rather than divide us apart, I think that's very, very important as well. And thinking about the working class, inequality, jobs, are first steps in that direction. Thank you very much, Daron. It was an honor for us. Oh, thank you. This has been great. Crusoe CEO Chase Lochmiller: Turning Stranded Energy Into Intelligence

Sequoia Capital(256.0K)·60:42·1.9K views·2026-10-29
Chase LochmillerCEO, Crusoe
TL;DRChase Lochmiller shares how Crusoe scaled to a $31 billion valuation by building a vertically integrated AI infrastructure platform modeled on oil and gas supermajors.
He discusses the realities of CEO evolution, the strategic divestment of their Bitcoin mining business, and managing the operational and community complexities of powering the age of intelligence. - Comprehensive Scope: Crusoe spans the entire stack from energy generation and...

TL;DR: Chase Lochmiller shares how Crusoe scaled to a $31 billion valuation by building a vertically integrated AI infrastructure platform modeled on oil and gas supermajors. He discusses the realities of CEO evolution, the strategic divestment of their Bitcoin mining business, and managing the operational and community complexities of powering the age of intelligence.

Scaling a Vertically Integrated AI Infrastructure Platform

  • Comprehensive Scope: Crusoe spans the entire stack from energy generation and power plants to large-scale AI data centers, manufacturing critical electrical equipment, and operating a managed AI cloud platform.
  • Organizational Complexity: Managing over 2,000 employees requires deep domain experts across energy, data centers, manufacturing, and AI cloud computing.
  • The Exxon Analogy: Crusoe models itself after oil and gas supermajors like Exxon and Chevron, categorized into upstream (energy generation), midstream (transport), and downstream (processing/compute), enabling natural resilience and co-opetition across the value chain.

The Evolution of a Founder CEO

  • The Boiling Frog Syndrome: Founder CEOs have the benefit of historical context, watching the company grow organically from two co-founders to a large organization rather than feeling paralyzed by sudden scale.
  • Continuous Personal Growth: Under Crusoe's core value of the "infinite growth loop," Lochmiller emphasized that no leader is a finished product and must continuously invest in themselves.
  • Executive Coaching and Public Speaking: Lochmiller credited his coach, Shane Metcalfe, with acting as a trusted guide and therapist through personnel challenges, and noted that he had to work exceptionally hard to become a polished public speaker.

Strategic Pivot: From Bitcoin Mining to AI

  • Energy-First Thesis: The original business thesis was bringing compute to stranded energy (such as flared natural gas in North Dakota's Bakken oil field) rather than moving energy to market.
  • Bitcoin as a Flywheel: Bitcoin mining served as the initial monetization engine to turn stranded energy into cash, creating a profitable foundation.
  • Divestment for Focus: Crusoe divested its Bitcoin mining business to Nidec (partnering with Ross Stevens and CEO Tejas Shah) to eliminate investor friction around crypto exposure and achieve total focus on category-defining AI infrastructure.

Energy Innovation and Diversification

  • Voracious Power Demand: To activate megawatts for intelligence, Crusoe utilizes a diverse energy mix including natural gas, behind-the-wind, solar, batteries, geothermal, and hydropower in Iceland and Norway.
  • Next-Gen Nuclear and Storage: Partnerships include Blue Energy for ocean-based nuclear and steam turbine technology, Aalo for SMR-powered AI factories at the Idaho National Lab, and long-duration storage companies like Form Energy and Entoura.

Leadership, Culture, and Crisis Management

  • Startups Die from Suicide: Rejecting paranoia about competitors, Lochmiller noted that most startups fail from internal execution failures, poor culture, or lack of focus rather than homicide by competitors.
  • Firing Speed: Acknowledging that leaders almost always fire too slowly, Lochmiller views personnel decisions as complex, snowflake cases where companies scale exponentially while people scale linearly.
  • Community Relations and Marketing: Facing public pushback and political debate around data center power and water usage, Lochmiller stresses that the industry has a marketing problem and must rely on transparent, first-principled dialogue with local communities.
  • Mountaineering Resilience: Drawing from his successful 2018 Mount Everest summit (after surviving the 2014 Khumbu Icefall avalanche), Crusoe institutionalized "Think Like a Mountaineer" as a core value emphasizing rigorous planning, preparation, and conscious risk management.

Notable Quotes

"Most startups die from suicide, not from homicide. It's not other businesses that kill you. It's you fundamentally not executing on the thing that's in front of you." — Chase Lochmiller
"We're trying to build an AI supermajor in the same way that Exxon and Chevron are supermajors in the oil and gas industry." — Chase Lochmiller
"Your job is to be the receiver of bad news very frequently and then be able to think with a clear mind and make decisions in light of that bad news." — Chase Lochmiller

And one kind of comparison that I've often made is that, you know, I I I kind of view this compute infrastructure layer of AI and or or SI, as people are calling it now today, this infrastructure layer of superintelligence is really, there's a lot of effective comparisons to the oil and gas industry. And if you look at if you look at the value chain across the oil and gas industry, you know, it's typically broken down into three components. You have the upstream business where people are actually drilling holes in the ground and and and pumping wells of oil and gas. You have the midstream business, which is where you're taking those those raw commodities and transporting them from the well head to the the the the the natural gas processing plant or the oil refinery. And then you have the downstream business, which is actually processing those raw outputs from the earth and converting them into usable materials, whether it's gasoline or jet fuel or plastics or, you know, whatever it is and then you're ultimately selling that into the market. And within that stack, there's all these different margins that move up and down depending on the prices of oil, depending on the demand from consumers, depending on the demand from businesses and for us, I view it as like, we're trying to build an AI supermajor in the same way that Exxon and Chevron are supermajors in the oil and gas industry and with that, you sort of have this embedded risk risk management, where, you know, Exxon very famously doesn't hedge their oil exposure because when oil prices come down, their upstream business at the wellhead, those margins actually get compressed, but their downstream business at the refinery, those margins actually expand because the raw input costs costs actually go down. And you know, they they they have these natural areas of collaboration versus competition where they're competing with other upstream businesses, but when they have a pipeline in an area, they're serving other upstream businesses that they may compete with. And I think Crusoe's, you know, kind of similar in that regard.
