And like when I recruit and I fund CEOs, that's definitely a quality that I look for. I liked how he talked about just how much the CEO job changed, he had never done anything like that before. He's eight years into the journey and man he's a learning machine, he read a lot about it. He hired a coach. It sounds like his coach was really useful. And I coach a bunch of Sequoia CEOs and you know, some of it is giving them advice, but a lot of it is gaining context and listening to the problems and empathizing and really kind of being like a therapist. So, he benefited a lot from that. The other thing he worked hard on, it sounds like his public speaking, he's very smooth. Like really articulate and smooth. And sounds like that wasn't his the default setting. He's had to really work at that. And I think that's important, like if if you're up in front of your company, you're up in front of investors, I don't think it hurts to be articulate and smooth. I like the way he talked about competition. He said something we used to say at Hubspot all the time, you're much more likely to die of suicide than murder. It was very much our case. It sounds to me he's really into co-opetition and I think a lot of founders frown on that. We had major co-opetition over many, many years with salesforce.com. So, I wouldn't shy away from that. The other thing I always did is I always got to know our competitors, just because you never know. And so don't, don't be afraid to reach out to your to your competitors and have coffee. The last thing I took that I really liked is as the organization gets bigger and I felt this at Hubspot, everyone wants to give you good news, you're the CEO and they expect you want to hear good news. And you really want the opposite, you want to hear the bad news and you want to train the organization to give you that bad news, so almost encouraging people when they push back at you and when they give you bad news inside of a, you know, a pretty good sized setting. I think that's a really important quality in CEOs. The other thing that I think is an important CEO quality that I think he does and you might too but he has very strong opinions as you've heard, but if the facts change, he's willing to change his mind and being stubborn is a really tough quality in a CEO and makes you quite hard to follow. Anyway, those are some of my takeaways. I thought he was great. It's an amazing business, he's an amazing CEO. I hope you liked it and I'll see you next time on Long Strange Trip.

Do You Trust Apple? | Stratechery by Ben Thompson

Stratechery(18.8K)·19:00·2.7K views·2026-10-29
Ben ThompsonHost, Stratechery
TL;DRApple’s new HomeView smart home ecosystem and hardware lineup highlight a fundamental question of consumer trust, balancing the company's strengths in privacy and seamless second-party integrations against historical product abandonments and lagging AI performance.
The HomeView Product: Features a design utilizing an A18 processor and 8GB of memory, with a development timeline that suffered nearly two years of delays due to Apple's AI crisis. Includes use cases such as displaying family calendars and photos (similar to DAKboard),...

TL;DR

Apple’s new HomeView smart home ecosystem and hardware lineup highlight a fundamental question of consumer trust, balancing the company's strengths in privacy and seamless second-party integrations against historical product abandonments and lagging AI performance.

Apple’s Smart Home Vision & The Home Collection

  • The HomeView Product:
    • Features a design utilizing an A18 processor and 8GB of memory, with a development timeline that suffered nearly two years of delays due to Apple's AI crisis.
    • Includes use cases such as displaying family calendars and photos (similar to DAKboard), programming thermostats, viewing video doorbells, checking security cameras, and hands-free access in locations like the kitchen.
  • Apple Home Collection Partnerships:
    • Designed in collaboration with LG Electronics and Schneider Electric for indoor/outdoor cameras, thermostats, temperature sensors, video doorbells, and door locks.
    • Seamless Setup & Privacy: Accessories offer one-tap setup with an iPhone via the Apple Home app with no separate app or account required, and are end-to-end encrypted.
    • Product Availability: Rollout begins in December, with select accessories launching in early 2027.

Historical Parallels & The Apple Trust Dividend

  • AirPort Precedent:
    • At Macworld New York on July 21, 1999, Steve Jobs introduced AirPort (Wi-Fi), highlighting an 11 megabits per second wireless LAN built in partnership with Lucent Technologies over 18 months.
    • Demonstrated that Apple’s core selling proposition has long been combining modern, easy-to-use technology with strong privacy protection.
  • The Trust Dividend:
    • Consumers trust Apple to deliver products that work together without the headache of flaky third-party developer software.
    • Apple provides a strong privacy umbrella, mitigating concerns regarding partners like LG Electronics' sketchy privacy track record.

Apple’s Trust Deficit & Past Missteps

  • Wireless Router Abandonment:
    • Apple disbanded its wireless router team over the past year leading up to 2018 (having not refreshed routers since 2013) to focus on consumer products generating the bulk of its revenue.
    • Left customers in limbo and demonstrated that Apple will abandon integrated infrastructure products if they do not move the financial needle relative to the iPhone.
  • Services Monetization:
    • Cameras in the Apple Home Collection require a paid iCloud+ plan for recorded event history, aligning with Apple's push into recurring subscription revenue.
    • Apple Music is natively supported on HomeView, while using Spotify requires a recently unlocked iPhone, which some view as a betrayal of trust.

The Siri AI Crisis & Competitive Landscape

  • Development Delays:
    • HomeView was originally aimed for release as early as the end of 2024, but faced a nearly two-year saga of delays caused by the lack of a working, revamped Siri assistant capable of retrieving personal content and recognizing household members via facial recognition.
  • AI Capabilities & Opportunity Cost:
    • The 2026 version of Siri AI is slower and makes more mistakes on basic tasks compared to deterministic older versions, trailing advanced agent alternatives like Muse, Instinct, Dots, and Grok Bot.
    • Committing to HomeView creates a heavy opportunity cost, locking users into a world controlled by Siri AI rather than rival AI systems.

Tim Cook’s Tenure, The Cook Doctrine, and Future Outlook

  • Leadership Transition & Financial Scale:
    • Tim Cook recently stepped down after taking Apple from 1 to $436 billion in revenue and $118 billion in profit.
    • However, critics question whether Cook's focus on revenue and services compromised elements of the Cook Doctrine—which states Apple must "own and control the primary technologies behind the products we make"—noting Apple does not own or control the models underlying Siri.
  • Strategic Partnerships:
    • Apple is leaning on partnerships akin to its historical Lucent collaboration, potentially delivering experiences via partnerships with Google and Meta (via Meta Superintelligence Lab).
    • The primary task for incoming leadership under John Ternus is ensuring Apple's "ease-of-use" technology transitions from merely easy to genuinely useful.
Do you trust Apple? On July 21, 1999, Steve Jobs did something remarkable at Macworld New York: first he went to Apple.com, then to “CNN Interactive”, then to Disney.com. That wasn’t the remarkable part: what blew the crowd away was when he picked up the just-introduced iBook and walked around while waiting for Disney.com to load. The crowd went wild at the lack of wires. I’m not going to quote this entire product introduction, but I’ve clipped the whole thing for your enjoyment; here are a few pertinent parts of Jobs’ introduction of what he called “AirPort”, and what the rest of the industry called “WIFI”: “But there is one more thing.” The main point of this keynote was the aforementioned introduction of the original iBook. This meant that “one more thing” was in fact not just an extra unexpected product, but an explicit addition to the primary product’s feature set. “No wire.” “No wire.” “What is going on here?” “We are really excited to announce, in addition to iBook, AirPort wireless networking… It’s a wireless LAN. It runs at 11 megabits per second, and it’s based on industry standards. This is 802.11 wireless networking, and everybody is jumping on board with this thing over the next six to nine months. So all sorts of devices are going to be able to interact with AirPorts. We’re just going to be there first and best, and the specification also incorporates quite a bit of privacy protection, so everything you send out is encrypted. A lot of privacy. We’ve worked with Lucent on this for the last 18 months, hand-in-hand to marry their wireless technology with our ease-of-use technology and to bring the cost of this down for people, because this stuff has been pretty pricey to date.” Apple’s selling proposition has, in many respects, never changed: modern technology that is easy to use and private too. “So AirPort, what is it? This is the first thing; this is the AirPort base station, and it looks like this, right like that, and so the AirPort base station is really cool… Well, iBook is the first computer ever designed right from the start to be optimized for wireless communications, and so right in every iBook are two built-in antennas… Then, we have an AirPort card, a small little card, and the iBook is designed to accept it. So let’s take a look at that. As you know, Apple pioneered this way of adding memory without taking your whole portable apart through simply lifting the keyboard. Well, we have enhanced that now to take an AirPort card, and as you see, there’s a space right there in every iBook ready for an AirPort card, and you can drop one in, just like that, and connect the cable that comes from the antenna. You saw that? I’ll go back. Just connect that cable and push it in, and now your iBook is AirPort functioning. You are on the wireless network. All the software detects the AirPort card and automatically sets it up for wireless networking without going through hours of configuring your computer. It all happens automatically. This has been a major effort on the part of Apple’s hardware and software engineers and also Lucent.” This paragraph is a bit extraneous, other than the fact it is personally meaningful: my first Apple computer was an iMac G4, the following all-white generation, with the same AirPort card installation. I remember being amazed at just how easy it was to add wireless. And now, here I am, 22 years later, still using an Apple laptop to write this Article. Two weeks ago I wondered how long I was for the Apple ecosystem; one product I was definitely not interested in was the then-forthcoming Apple smarthome product. What struck me about Gurman’s article is just how unenthused I am by an Apple smarthome product. Some of this is fatigue from a decade of Siri disappointment and skepticism about the company’s ability to deliver on a voice-centric product. More than that, however, I bristle at the idea of introducing Apple’s constraints to more parts of my life. Those constraints aren’t just about things like full disk access. To the extent that Apple delivers on integration with things like thermostats and door locks is the extent to which they work with 3rd-party device makers; the problem is that third party device makers mostly suck, particularly from a software perspective. Even if Siri were perfect, Apple will have the challenge of delivering an experience that isn’t defined by the lowest common denominator. What I’m much more interested in is controlling the software layer myself. The fact of the matter is that with AI you can decompile almost all existing software — there is a revolution happening in gaming over the past few weeks, as game after game is decompiled to source and ported to any platform you wish — and you can write your own. That means my software that interacts with my agent in the way I want it to for everything. That’s way more exciting than praying Apple delivers the right API and that 3rd-party developers don’t suck. That product is called Apple HomeView, and some prominent Apple observers don’t get the point. John Gruber wrote on Daring Fireball: “My skepticism boils down to the question of why anyone would want to add one (or more!) additional ever-present screens to our homes and lives.” Phones can stay in our pockets until we need them. Watches remain on our wrists. HomePods can be tucked away unobtrusively in corners, and remain fully useful. HomeView needs to be in a prominent location for its display to serve any practical purpose. Apple’s initial pitch for it doesn’t earn that prominence for me. I understand Gruber’s skepticism, particularly given the product’s truly atrocious introductory video. Beyond the overall stress-inducing nature of the suburban family scene depicted, most of the use cases, as Gruber notes, are nonsensical: to take a particularly egregious example, why would the teenage daughter look at a message from her clandestine boyfriend on the HomeView instead of her iPhone (which has to be present on the network and unlocked in the past several hours for that functionality to even work)? Apple was, in this particular case, trying to show off HomeView’s multi-user capability, which is based on facial recognition, but my biggest takeaway is that maybe highly produced marketing videos sold as product keynotes aren’t the worst idea in the world. In fact, I think that HomeView is pretty compelling. Smarthome functionality is genuinely useful: Years ago I set up a small monitor on the side of our condo’s living room for the kids to play video games; I happened to have a Raspberry Pi on hand, which I used to set up a service called DAKboard. DAKboard showed the time, the weather, the family calendar, and a rotating collection of photos. It has, in the intervening years, been one of our most-used and most-beloved pieces of technology, looked at multiple times a day every day. Thermostats are finicky and hard to use; it’s much better to be able to program them from your phone, or from a touch device. The problem for me in my current home is that my thermostats seem to mysteriously lose their connection to my home automation system every few weeks. Doorballs are critical in a world of e-commerce, especially when it comes to the dreaded “signature required”; incorporating them with your devices, along with a screen to see who is at the door, is a no-brainer. Unfortunately for me my doorbells also seem to get randomly disconnected, and I never notice until I miss a delivery. Cameras are a useful security measure, but only if you can actually view them. The issue is — you guessed it — that they often become inaccessible or are non-intuitive to use, particularly the moment you need them. Sometimes I want to access information without using my hands, like when I am cooking. You can prop up an iPhone or iPad in the kitchen, but what do you do when it goes to sleep in the middle of a recipe? I have, of course, just laid out five compelling use cases for the HomeView, but it wasn’t hard! These are all real, genuinely useful capabilities for a new home device. And notice what the limiting factor is for the middle three: actually getting different devices to work together reliably. That’s why the other exciting part about Apple’s HomeView announcement actually hearkened back to the original AirPort: “Apple today announced Apple Home Collection, a new lineup of smart home accessories — including indoor and outdoor cameras, thermostats, temperature sensors, and a video doorbell and door lock designed in collaboration with LG Electronics and Schneider Electric. These new accessories are simple to set up starting with one tap with iPhone, and everything is managed through the Apple Home app — no separate app or account required. Each accessory is encrypted end-to-end in Apple Home, so only users and the people they choose can access their home data. Building on the thousands of Works with Apple Home and Matter accessories available today, Apple Home Collection offers customers a new array of recommended products to better understand what’s happening inside and outside their home, manage access, and keep the temperature just right throughout the day. Availability for all products begins in December, with select accessories available starting early 2027. “Apple Home Collection makes it easy to choose smart home accessories with confidence,” said Matthew Costello, Apple’s vice president of Hardware Engineering. “Working closely with industry leaders LG Electronics and Schneider Electric, we designed every accessory to work seamlessly with Apple Home, so managing your home is simple, reliable, and private.” Apple doesn’t have to do everything itself in the home, just as they didn’t have to invent wireless networking in 1999; what they need to do is “marry technology with ease-of-use technology”, and I’m optimistic they’ve done exactly that with LG Electronics and Schneider Electric. The key detail is “no separate app or account required”; that points to deep integration with Apple products, from setup to day-to-day usage. Again, I’m not sure I’m the target customer for this (although the automatically unlocking door locks look awesome); my solution to incompatible or flakey integrations is to use AI to build my own customized software. I’m under no illusions, however, that I am a normal consumer who wants the benefits of technology without the headaches; I trust Apple, more than anyone else, to at least aspire to delivering on that vision, and these 2nd-party partnerships are a great start. The second part where you may trust Apple more than any other company is with privacy. Jobs was talking about privacy as a selling point 27 years ago, and Apple has only doubled down since then. In the case of HomeView specifically there is that facial recognition feature, tied to your own hardware; more broadly, if there is any company you might entrust with tracking your more private spaces it is Apple, both because of promises they make with their technology and also their consistent stance on not using customer data. Indeed, Apple’s privacy umbrella is strong enough that LG’s very sketchy privacy track record is likely to be a non-issue amongst potential customers. “Apple Inc. has disbanded its division that develops wireless routers, another move to try to sharpen the company’s focus on consumer products that generate the bulk of its revenue, according to people familiar with the matter. Apple began shutting down the wireless router team over the past year, dispersing engineers to other product development groups, including the one handling the Apple TV, said the people, who asked not to be named because the decision hasn’t been publicly announced. Apple hasn’t refreshed its routers since 2013 following years of frequent updates to match new standards from the wireless industry. The decision to disband the team indicates the company isn’t currently pushing forward with new versions of its routers. An Apple spokeswoman declined to comment on the company’s plans.” The official end of the line came in 2018, five years after the last product was released, leaving customers in limbo waiting for updates that never came. That right there is one reason to not trust Apple in the home: the company already decided once that products that didn’t move the needle relative to the iPhone weren’t worth their time, even if delivering an integrated home experience was still something Apple was uniquely positioned to provide. Yes, by the 2010s most people had WiFi access points from their ISPs, or mesh WiFi products from the likes of Eero, but Apple still could have done something useful. For example, one of the biggest challenges in designing a wireless network is that the iPhone aggressively holds onto access points; if Apple had continued to design its own access points it could, even today, provide a superior experience. Alas, the business was deemed to be not big enough to bother. One reason Apple might be more interested this time, however, is the possibility of subscription revenue. Specifically, the cameras in Apple Home Collection will require a paid iCloud+ plan for recorded event history — higher levels for more cameras — which fits in with the company’s focus on services. Apple Music is also natively supported on the HomeView, whereas you’ll need a recently unlocked iPhone to use Spotify. Of course to some this might be another betrayal of trust: Apple used to sell you products, now they want to sell you on-ramps to recurring revenue. Both of these issues of trust, however, pale in comparison to the single biggest question facing HomeView: do you trust Siri to actually work? “Apple Inc.’s new HomeView has been a long time coming — far longer than the company intended. I first reported on the device in January 2023, when Apple was aiming to bring it to market as early as the end of 2024. What should have been a relatively straightforward launch instead turned into a nearly two-year saga of delays. The culprit was Apple’s AI crisis, which left the company without the one piece of technology the HomeView absolutely needed: a working version of its revamped Siri assistant… From the beginning, the HomeView was built around a Siri that could retrieve personal content, distinguish between members of a household and show each person private information. It was also designed to be controlled primarily by voice, rather than through its touch screen. Without a serviceable version of the new assistant, Apple couldn’t deliver the experience it had envisioned. That left the hardware ready to go, but the software nowhere near finished. So Apple postponed the HomeView repeatedly — first until spring 2025, then summer, then fall, and then again throughout 2026 before finally unveiling it this past week. And the hardware betrays its age. With thick bezels, an A18 processor and 8 gigabytes of memory, this is clearly a product conceived years ago. Finished units have been sitting boxed up in warehouses for the better part of a year, waiting for the software to catch up.” There is a world where Apple never gives up on AirPort, and instead uses it as a foundation to deliver the integrated smarthome in the late 2010s; in that world Siri’s performance is important but also less pressing given that some number of homes would already be locked in. The second world, which Gurman describes, is one where Apple delivers on the new Siri in 2024 like it planned, and the HomeView is its showpiece experience. This is still a compelling product in that scenario, where integrating information from your iPhone is close to the cutting edge of what AI is capable of. The world we are in in 2026, however, is a much different one. In this world Siri AI has shipped, and while it is more capable than what came before, it is much slower, and makes more mistakes with basic tasks that the deterministic old Siri handled reliably. Moreover, the competitive landscape is dramatically different: agents like Muse, Instinct, Dots, or Grok Bot not only integrate information but actually act on it; new Siri already feels long in the tooth. This is where a foray into the home is particularly challenging. One doesn’t replace one’s thermostat or cameras or doorbells every year; these are one-time purchases meant to last for a long time. Ditto cutting a hole in your wall to mount a HomeView, which certainly makes more sense for many of the product’s core use cases. Making such a commitment, however, means committing to Siri AI as the primary interface, and trusting that Apple will make it good. This is a costly choice, and not just monetarily. The real concern is opportunity cost: committing to a world controlled by Siri AI means forgoing a world controlled by any other AI. That’s too expensive for me; how many consumers, even those who trust Apple to make things work together, and who value the company’s commitment to privacy, will make a similar evaluation? The answer right now, in 2026, is probably not too many, which is to say Apple might have got this product out just in time. I think there is something here, even if Apple’s marketing team doesn’t seem to be able to explain it very well. At the same time, I think HomeView highlights some of the mistakes and shortcomings of former CEO Tim Cook’s tenure; earlier this year when Cook retired I wrote about his impeccable timing, both in terms of when he took over Apple, and also when he stepped down: “To that end, it’s right that Cook is stepping down now. Jobs might have been responsible for taking Apple from 0 to 1, but it was Cook that took Apple from 1 to $436 billion in revenue and $118 billion in profit last year. It’s a testament to his capabilities and execution that Apple didn’t suffer any sort of post-founder hangover; only time will tell if, along the way, Cook created the conditions for a crash out, by virtue of he himself forgetting The Cook Doctrine and what makes Apple Apple.” The Cook Doctrine states that Apple needs “to own and control the primary technologies behind the products we make”, but Apple doesn’t own and control the models underlying Siri; meanwhile, one of the things that makes Apple Apple is focusing on making great products that customers love. How did Apple’s obsession with services revenue fit in that rubric, particularly when said obsession rose in conjunction with stripping out every part of the business that didn’t move the needle in terms of revenue, even if it made for a better user experience? Privacy, meanwhile, is great as a strategy credit; what happens when it gets in the way of making products that work? It is true that Apple’s AI approach has the benefit of not moving the needle in terms of costs, at least relative to its big tech peers. It’s possible that just as Apple is partnering with LG Electronics and Schneider Electric on home devices, the company will deliver compelling experiences through its partnership with Google (and surprising thawing with Meta via its Meta Superintelligence Lab). After all, Apple hasn’t always done everything — Lucent Technologies once featured in a Steve Jobs keynote! The difference is that that was an Apple that was eager to race ahead, not simply catch up; Jobs was planting seeds for the future, seeds that Cook harvested. Cook did make investments that matter, particularly in Apple Silicon (even if that effort started under Jobs), and in wearables; the job for Ternus will be ensuring that Apple’s “ease-of-use” technology is not just easy but actually useful.

Franklin Templeton CEO on SpaceX, Anthropic, Circular Deals & the State of the Market

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(77.5K)·42:21·1.8K views·2026-10-29
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Jenny JohnsonCEO, Franklin Templeton
TL;DRFranklin Templeton CEO Jenny Johnson discusses navigating the current macroeconomic environment, managing late-stage venture investments in companies like SpaceX and Anthropic, and driving internal efficiencies through tokenization and AI agents.
Inflation and Interest Rates: Inflation remains sticky and rates are likely to stay higher; while unusual for the past 25 years, historical mortgage and rate levels show this is not unprecedented. Corporate & Consumer Strength: The US consumer remains strong, with roughly 70%...

TL;DR

Franklin Templeton CEO Jenny Johnson discusses navigating the current macroeconomic environment, managing late-stage venture investments in companies like SpaceX and Anthropic, and driving internal efficiencies through tokenization and AI agents.

State of the Market & Macro Environment

  • Inflation and Interest Rates: Inflation remains sticky and rates are likely to stay higher; while unusual for the past 25 years, historical mortgage and rate levels show this is not unprecedented.
  • Corporate & Consumer Strength: The US consumer remains strong, with roughly 70% of companies beating projected earnings.
  • Geopolitical Headwinds: Persistent conflicts in the Middle East (threatening oil transit through the Strait of Hormuz) and Russia-Ukraine are viewed as "business as usual."
  • US-China Relations: Characterized as a "frenemy" dynamic where tech domains will diverge, but mutual reliance on rare earths and consumer markets necessitates constructive cooperation.

AI Bubbles, Hyperscalers, & Infrastructure

  • Exuberance Curves: Markets repeatedly experience new technology hype cycles—surging to disillusionment before settling to realistic valuations—with AI currently showing frothiness in specific sectors.
  • Financing Horizons: Long-term financing for power-hungry data centers carries high risk due to the assumption that technology and energy needs will not change over the next 20 to 30 years.
  • Accounting & Revenue Clarity: Variations in revenue recognition and circular financing among hyperscalers require rigorous active management and caution.
  • Internal Governance Shifts: Long-term enterprise spending will likely face headwinds as organizations institute governance over agent frequency and route simple queries to open-source or free models rather than costly frontier models.

Private Markets & Late-Stage Venture Strategy

  • Late-Stage Venture Entry: Due to companies staying private longer, Franklin Templeton leveraged its Silicon Valley headquarters and venture ecosystem to invest in late-stage deals.
  • Key Portfolio Exposures: The firm holds late-stage investments in high-profile companies including SpaceX and Anthropic.
  • SpaceX Assessment: Praised for diversified strategies (Starlink, data center contracts, and launch cost reductions), though balanced against "key man risk" regarding Elon Musk.

M&A and Scaling a $1.8T Asset Manager

  • Legg Mason Acquisition ($4.5B): Closed five days after Johnson became CEO and three weeks before COVID lockdowns; balanced the firm to a 50/50 split between institutional and wealth clients while adding core fixed income and real estate (Clarion Partners).
  • Six-Year M&A Track Record: Completed 17 acquisitions to broaden capabilities and distribution (including Lexington Partners for secondary private equity and Benefit Street Partners for private credit).
  • Asset Growth Post-Acquisition:
    • Canvas (direct indexing/OSAM): Grew from $2.5 billion to $30 billion in 4 years.
    • Lexington Partners and Clarion Partners: Both more than doubled in assets under management.
    • Benefit Street Partners (BSP): Expanded from $26 billion to just under $100 billion in private credit.
    • Putnam Investments: Grew assets from $136 billion to over $220 billion by leveraging platform access.

Tokenization & Financial Infrastructure

  • Blockchain Rails: Blockchain offers a single source of truth, immediate updates, elimination of costly reconciliation, and smart contract automation that will eventually replace legacy mainframe rails.
  • Tokenized Money Market Fund: Developed the first SEC-approved natively on-chain tokenized money market fund; parallel processing revealed massive cost efficiencies:
    • Lowers account opening minimums from $500 (traditional) to $20 (on-chain), with potential to drop to $5.
    • Calculates interest every second, enabling atomic settlement, 24/7 trading, and real-time interest payouts for corporate treasurers and hedge funds.

Enterprise AI & Internal Adoption

  • Sales and Distribution Gains: Partnered with Microsoft on a multi-agent AI system matching client holdings with product data; yielded a 23% uplift in client visits and an 11% increase in sales.
  • Workforce Deployment: Manages over 1,000 AI agents supporting more than 800 investment professionals to handle administrative tasks and code-writing pipelines.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Jenny Johnson predicts that blockchain rails will ultimately replace traditional financial services infrastructure, though adoption will be slow due to existing toll-taker business models.
  • Jenny Johnson predicts that long-term financing commitments for data centers carry high risk because they rely on the assumption that chip and energy technology will not change dramatically over the next 20 to 30 years.

Notable Quotes

"If you're betting on the needs for energy based on today's technology, both on the manufacturing of chips as well as the production of energy, you're assuming that for the next 20 or 30 years, it doesn't change dramatically. But that's pretty unusual." — Jenny Johnson
"I genuinely believe [blockchain] will replace the rails in financial services. It'll be slow because it forces the change of a bunch of business models and the toll takers who who take a fee on a transaction." — Jenny Johnson
"There is no person who's had more significant impact, um, on really where we are in the technological advances today, than Elon has." — Jenny Johnson (comparing Elon Musk to a modern-day Benjamin Franklin)
Your grandfather started in 1947, managing 2.5 million in AUM to now 1.8 trillion in AUM. Done 17 acquisitions in the last 6 years. I think the canvas application was about 2.5 billion when we acquired them and it's now 30 billion. I found out that you guys had exposure to SpaceX. We did. We did, Anthropic, I mean a bunch of them. Yes. Well, I think the biggest growth lever is AI. It is such a massive disruptor. There is no person who's had more significant impact on really where we are in the technological advances today than Elon has. Ken Griffin was just on stage for the Citadel Securities conference and of course they asked him about the AI bubble. Maybe this is a bubble. There's almost certainly bubbles within this moment in time. It happens over and over. New technology is the greatest thing ever, it's going to be the greatest thing, it goes all the way up, it's so great, it's so great, boom and everybody's disillusioned by it, it's terrible, and then it comes back up to where it sort of should be. So the question is, where are we on that curve? Jenny Johnson, welcome to Sourcery. Thanks for having me. It's great to be here. Thanks for having me in your super fancy gorgeous new New York City office. You're very exciting and you're in the heritage room which is all about our history. I know, you guys like Benjamin Franklin, huh? We we do kind of like him. He was he was a stud. He was a pretty cool guy. Yeah. So, I'm going to ask you a question about him later, but before we begin, I just want to give a nod to Franklin Templeton. You guys have been around for multi-generations. Your grandfather started in 1947. You now, you went from managing 2.5 million in AUM to now 1.8 trillion in AUM, which is crazy. It's a huge growth, there's a huge growth story behind it, and for your story in particular as well, like you started in the mailroom and now you're the CEO. So, I want to talk through all of that, but before we get to it, where are we in the state of the market? We have people saying we're in a hidden bear market. There's also all this fun about AI and the premiums there, and the big deals that are happening, the IPOs that are being pushed back. So, how do you kind of consider this? Another part of that is tokenization. We haven't talked about tokenization yet. I know you've talked about this a bunch. So, what did you see with tokenization? Again, having run technology and operations, I think that an advantage in the seat on understanding why blockchain was so important, and I genuinely believe it will replace the rails in financial services. It'll it'll be slow because it forces the change of a bunch of business models and the toll takers who who take a fee on a transaction. But it it basically does three things really well. It has a source of truth, and anybody who runs a back office or tech department understands, we're still running, well we're not, but a lot of big financial services firms still run mainframes, which means you have batch processing at night, which means your data is not updated until sometime in the middle of the night. With blockchain, it's immediate. And so, there's a huge cost in firms to reconcile data between systems, and then you have to go reconcile the transaction with your counterparty. That all goes away with blockchain. There's just one source of truth, updated immediately that everybody goes to. The second thing it has the ability to program these smart contracts. So, whatever terms you and I agree, if this threshold meets you have to pay me collateral or this one I have to pay you collateral, all of that gets programmed in, whereas pre-blockchain, that is sitting in a document somewhere, or you have to go and, you know, try to build it into the transaction, but it's really hard to do. So, it tends to be in a document that somebody's following and they'll say, "Oh, let me go collect collateral you owe to me." That goes away. Immediately is paid and the third thing is the ability to make a payment. So, I looked at that and said, "Well, this is a no brainer. This is what we do in this business in the back office, and so, this is hugely efficient." And when we came out with the, first tokenized natively on chain tokenized money market fund that the SEC approved, they had us parallel process on our old transfer agency system, our old shareholder recordkeeping system and our blockchain system, and a couple things, we were still one of the few firms, I think there were only two of us still running the old system in-house. And so, we were able to look at the parallel processing, and we were astonished by how much more cost effective it was to run it on chain. And we looked at that, like if you open one of our traditional money market funds, you need $500 to open an account, because anything below that, the other shareholders are subsidizing your servicing fees. Whereas, we did it on the blockchain system, and you can open an account for $20. We probably can go down to $5, because it's just that much more efficient to run. So, we look at it and think that the financial innovation that's going to happen, is going to be really interesting and significant. One is you're going to have 24/7 trading, it's going to be atomic settlement, and you're going to have a lot of innovation that's going to happen because of the ability to program, and now add AI to that, you can build your logic of a trading pattern in there. And so, we just, we're, we're big fans. Our tokenized money market fund, to give you an idea, it calculates interest every second. If you're a hedge fund and you use it as collateral and you hold it for four hours, 22 minutes and 32 seconds, we'll pay you that interest. Your cash that you earn that day is paid in your account at the end of the day. On a traditional money market fund, it's accrued all month, but you can't get to that cash until the end of the month. And you know, if you're a corporate treasurer, that's kind of significant. GTC Berlin 2026: Power Demand, Increasing Grid Reliability and Resiliency

NVIDIA(2.3M)·41:24·56 views·2026-10-29
Egon WesterExecutive, Bayernwerk
TL;DREuropean energy leaders from TenneT, Bayernwerk, and Siemens Energy discuss how AI, accelerated computing, and data-driven digital twins are modernizing the power grid, managing massive decentralization, and combating escalating physical and cyber threats as demand surges.
Decentralized Scale: Bayernwerk (Egon Wester): Operates lines and cables going more than 4 times around the world, covering roughly 10% of the German electricity system in the south. Massive Growth: Transitioned from ~40 big power plants 15 years ago to more than 1 million...

TL;DR

European energy leaders from TenneT, Bayernwerk, and Siemens Energy discuss how AI, accelerated computing, and data-driven digital twins are modernizing the power grid, managing massive decentralization, and combating escalating physical and cyber threats as demand surges.

Grid Modernization & The Decentralization Challenge

  • Decentralized Scale:
    • Bayernwerk (Egon Wester): Operates lines and cables going more than 4 times around the world, covering roughly 10% of the German electricity system in the south.
    • Massive Growth: Transitioned from ~40 big power plants 15 years ago to more than 1 million renewable generation plants today, making it the most decentralized energy system in Europe (and home to more residential storage systems than California, despite California having 5 times the area).
  • Transmission Highway:
    • TenneT (Catherine): Operates the largest grid network in Germany, transporting offshore and renewable energy from the North Sea down to the south of the country.
    • Investment Scale: Investing 12 billion euros per year in physical assets and substations; Egon Wester notes his company's current capital plan is 30 billion euros, heavily weighted toward copper-oriented infrastructure combined with smart technologies.
  • Global Demand Growth:
    • Siemens Energy (Adnan): While regions like Europe (renewables integration) and North America (conventional approach) had different historical journeys, every global market (Indonesia, India, Americas, Europe) now faces identical challenges around massive demand growth (data centers, industrialization, e-mobility) and limited, constrained capacity.

Security Threats & Hybrid Defense

  • Multidimensional Threats:
    • Physical & Cyber Convergence: Grids face a 3-part threat landscape spanning physical attacks (e.g., severe arson attacks on high-voltage poles in spring), sophisticated cyber threats/social engineering, and personnel safety.
    • Operational Disruptions: Egon Wester notes Bayernwerk suffered a cyber attack on a major dispatch center service provider that disconnected them for several months, followed by physical arson attacks on infrastructure.
  • AI-Powered Defense Systems:
    • Grid Secure & Drones: Developed in rapid response with Siemens Energy following the spring attacks, *Grid Secure* is embedded into overhead line equipment to observe surroundings. Bayernwerk also uses drone detection systems with a Bavarian partner.
    • Holistic Pattern Recognition: Catherine emphasizes that 100% physical protection of wide-area infrastructure is impossible; AI enables the cross-correlation of physical, cyber, and personal data to detect anomalies, spot early warning patterns, and counter disinformation campaigns.

Digital Twins & Cross-Industry Tech Transfer

  • Automotive Technology in the Grid:
    • Cross-Industry Adaptation: Siemens Energy leveraged AI libraries from self-driving car perception systems (which detect objects like children jumping into traffic) and embedded them into simulation engines and operational centers to protect critical infrastructure via the Noedra platform.
    • Rapid Prototyping: The collaborative security solution with Bayernwerk was built within 2 months.
  • Rigorous Digital Twin Development:
    • Cross-Disciplinary Teams: Building digital twins for transformers required experts from medical science, biochemical science, and weather simulation rather than traditional power systems.
    • Iterative Success: Siemens Energy went through 4 failed attempts before the 5th attempt successfully created the transformer digital twin, underscoring that energy digital twins require a fundamentally different approach than legacy simulation models.

Strategic Vision & 2030 Roadmap

  • Autonomous Grid Ambitions:
    • Siemens Energy: Targeting the provision of technology needed to run the grid highly autonomous (even fully autonomous) by 2030 and beyond, anchored by secure hardware and governed AI agents (joining NVIDIA's open agent security platform).
  • Cultural & Operational Transformation:
    • TenneT (Catherine): Emphasizes that every company becoming an AI company requires a cultural mindset shift in a traditional, regulated utility over the next 4 years, alongside upskilling the 6,000 employees who operate the network.
    • Bayernwerk (Egon Wester): Stresses the shift from predictable, planning-oriented operations to real-time operations centered on "Flexiumers" and grid flexibility rather than reserving static capacity for 8,760 hours a year.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Adnan (Siemens Energy): By 2030, Siemens Energy aims to provide all technology necessary to operate the electrical grid highly autonomously, and where possible, autonomously.

Notable Quotes

*"If you want to solve the challenge, you have to really think holistically and not now on one area... the car is a very big example—a self-driving car can detect if a kid is jumping in front of the car, can process this... we need to get these kind of technologies into the grid."* — Adnan (Siemens Energy)
*"We are a regulated energy company, but the tendency that technical possibilities make us more and more capable to introduce automatic decision processes... is there. But the limit is the willing to trust them and the clear responsibility given by the authorities."* — Egon Wester (Bayernwerk)
*"As Jensen said this morning, every company is getting an AI company. I think this is the kind of mindset we have to implement also into a traditional regulated energy company."* — Catherine (TenneT)
