How an unelected man ruled New York for 30 years

Dwarkesh Patel(1.5M)·24:42·21.9K views·2026-10-29
Dwarkesh PatelHost, Dwarkesh Patel Robert CaroAuthor
TL;DRRobert Moses and Lyndon Johnson mastered the accumulation and exercise of unelected political power through legislative engineering, control over financial and institutional leverage, and masterclass coalition-building.
Writing Your Own Authority: Moses drafted the laws that governed him, notably the Triborough Bridge Authority Act of 1933, embedding obscure subsections that allowed him to perpetually refinance bonds for entirely new corporate purposes. Constitutional Protections: By tying...

TL;DR

Robert Moses and Lyndon Johnson mastered the accumulation and exercise of unelected political power through legislative engineering, control over financial and institutional leverage, and masterclass coalition-building.

Robert Moses: The Master Builder and Legislative Architect

  • Writing Your Own Authority: Moses drafted the laws that governed him, notably the Triborough Bridge Authority Act of 1933, embedding obscure subsections that allowed him to perpetually refinance bonds for entirely new corporate purposes.
    • Constitutional Protections: By tying his powers directly into bond contracts, Moses made his authority immune to revocation by mayors, governors, or state legislatures under the U.S. Constitution's contract clause.
  • The Hydra Strategy: Moses held 12 government posts simultaneously—including City Construction Coordinator, Chairman of the State Council of Parks, and Chairman of the State Power Authority—preventing any single elected official from cutting off all his power bases at once.
  • Capital and Speed: Moses operated with unmatched financial capacity and execution speed, allowing him to deliver massive public works within single election cycles.
    • Federal Funding Dominance: By maintaining the largest team of planners and engineers in the country, New York captured 1/7th of all federal Depression-era work relief funds by 1936.
    • Debt Capacity: In 1946, the entire New York City government could only borrow $150 million before hitting its legal debt limit, whereas Moses's Triborough Authority could borrow $360 million in that same year.
  • The YIMBY Graft Machine: Moses built concentrated private-sector coalitions where banks bought discounted non-competitive bonds, unions secured jobs, connected lawyers collected fees, and party bosses brokered bridge insurance policies, creating a powerful defense network against political interference.

Lyndon Johnson: The Senate Dominator and Pragmatic Opportunist

  • Transforming the Majority Leadership: Entering the Senate when the position was considered a thankless, powerless lightning rod, Johnson turned the office into the second most powerful post in America using three core tactics.
  • Leverage and Rule Changes: Johnson stripped committee assignments away from strict seniority, centralizing control through the party's Steering Committee so he could reward allies and punish defectors.
    • The Kefauver Defiance: In 1957, after Estes Kefauver voted against Johnson on an important motion, a highly coveted open seat on the Foreign Relations Committee was stripped from Kefauver and handed to a junior senator, John F. Kennedy.
  • Agenda Setting via Unanimous-Consent: Johnson normalized the use of unanimous-consent agreements to bypass unlimited Senate debate, fixing debate times and amendment opportunities so every vote was predetermined through off-stage deals.
  • Masterful Coalition Brokerage: Johnson combined wholly unrelated legislative objectives to pass the Civil Rights Act of 1957, trading a western federal dam, a jury trial protection for Midwestern railroad union strikers, and federal jury rights for black citizens in exchange for keeping the bill weak enough to avoid a Southern filibuster.

Ideology vs. Power

  • Moses's Arc from Reform to Addiction: Moses began as a brilliant young idealist fighting Tammany Hall patronage in the 1910s, learning that ideals are useless without power, but ultimately became addicted to power for its own sake.
  • Johnson's Opportunistic Pragmatism: Early in his career, Johnson showed no fixed ideological commitments, molding himself to match the dominant Southern Dixiecrats before leveraging that hard-won power as President to champion the 1964 Civil Rights Act and the 1965 Voting Rights Act.

Notable Quotes

"Robert Moses was not himself corrupt. He was, in fact, as uninterested in obtaining payoffs for himself as any public servant who ever lived. In the politicians' phrase, he was 'money honest.'" — Robert Caro
"It was not unusual at park and playground opening ceremonies for children, prodded by their parents, to break into the cheer, '2-4-6-8, who do we appreciate? Mr. Moses, Mr. Moses, Mr. Moses!'" — Robert Caro
So, I recently interviewed Robert Caro. He spent the last 50 years writing a four-volume biography of Lyndon Johnson, and he spent another seven years before that writing the biography of Robert Moses, who was the master builder of New York. His books are widely regarded as the best explorations of political power that have ever been written, and they also happen to be literally my favorite books ever. Whenever I meet somebody new, one of the first questions that they often ask me is, "Who's the person you most want to interview that you haven't yet?" And for six years, I have answered that question in the same way: Robert Caro. And I've actually spent a lot of time trying to make this interview happen. I've chased down his agent, I've called his publisher, I've contacted the museums which are hosting his archives. I've even sent him handwritten letters. I finally got to sit down with him, and I'm very happy with how it turned out. It'll be out tomorrow. In the meantime, as I was preparing for this interview and I was rereading all of these biographies, I was writing down all the lessons that they laid out about how to accumulate and use power, and this video essay is the result. Robert Moses was the master builder of New York. He built more of the physical city than anybody before or since. He built the Triborough and Verrazzano Bridges, the Cross Bronx Expressway, Jones Beach, Lincoln Center, Shea Stadium, two giant power dams upstate, in addition to hundreds of parks and pools. Of the 899 miles of highways completed in New York state by 1964, Moses had built 672 of them. His projects collectively cost hundreds of billions of dollars in today's money. For more than 30 years, Robert Moses was the most powerful person in New York, more powerful than any elected mayor or governor, despite the fact that he was not elected to anything. How did he get that much power, and how did he keep it? Part of the answer is that Moses wrote the laws that governed him. He was called the best bill drafter in Albany, and he used that skill to write himself powers that the legislators voting on his bills didn't know that they were granting. The political genius of Robert Moses is perhaps best illustrated by his use of public authorities. These are government-created corporations that build city infrastructure. Public authorities sell bonds to pay for a specific project, say, a new bridge. Once the bridge is built, the authority would charge drivers a toll until it had collected enough money to pay off its bonds. Then, the authority would tear down the toll booths, hand the bridge over to the city, and go out of existence. At least, that was the idea. And that's what Albany assumed would happen when the New York State Congress approved the Triborough Bridge Authority Act of 1933, whose final version included, buried within pages and pages of legalese, a few seemingly minor alterations that were drafted by Robert Moses. Now, Section 1 of this act clearly stated that the authority would dissolve once the bonds were paid off. And since the bonds could run at most 40 years, and the revenues from the bridge would pay back the initial cost far sooner, it seemed like the authority had a clear built-in expiry date. However, in a subsection of Section 9, Moses added a single sentence that said that the authority could, quote, from time to time, end quote, pay off its old bonds by issuing new ones. And those new ones could be, quote, for any other corporate purpose, end quote. So, just before the original bonds were about to expire, the authority now had the right to issue a totally fresh 40-year note for a potentially totally different use. And what could the authority build with all this money? Well, the original act let it build two bridges and their approaches. Triborough's chairman, Robert Moses, called this project more than a mere bridge. It will be, he said, an immense artery of travel in the very heart of the city. Round the island, through the toll gate, rides the first vehicle. Young men in rented tuxedos, the passengers. They parked here all week for the honor of being first to cross. Moses's amendments also gave this authority the right to build connecting roads and, quote, facilities for the public, not inconsistent with the use of the project, end quote. These rights, when interpreted as creatively as Moses intended to, would allow Moses to alter most of the roads, parks, and housing in New York. For for a road, connected to a road, which connected to a road, which connected to the bridge. Or for a park, lay along the road that connected to the road that connected to the road that connected to the bridge. Surely the Triborough Authority was entitled to improve and change it. All this construction cost could be financed by the bridge's enormous toll revenues, and the right of the Triborough Authority to borrow against them. To give some perspective, in 1946, the entire New York City government could only borrow $150 million before hitting its legal debt limit. That very same year, Triborough could borrow $360 million. So, Moses's Bridge Authority had access to more than double the amount of money as the largest city in America. And what if a future governor or mayor eventually learned about all this power and revenue that had been delegated to this authority and they decided to claw it back? Moses had planned for this too, and his solution was so clever that no lawyer or lawmaker in Albany saw it coming. The United States Constitution says that no state shall make, quote, any law impairing the obligation of contracts, end quote, entered into by government entity. A bond issued by a public authority is such a contract. Moses snuck a clause into the act which allowed him to add, quote, any other matters of like or different character, which in any way affect the security or protection of the bonds, end quote, into these bond contracts. And these matters included that Triborough could charge whatever tolls it wanted, and for however long they wanted, and they could keep the surplus thus generated. That is to say, Moses could now entrench his powers simply by writing them into the bonds that he issued. From the moment that those bonds were sold, those powers could no longer be revoked by the legislature, or by the governor, or by the mayor. By anything, in fact, short of a constitutional convention. The powers that Moses now had were guaranteed for the bond's entire lifetime, a lifetime which would always be extended simply by refinancing. So, with just a few minor tweaks, Moses had turned an act meant to allow for the construction of two bridges into the basis of an institution that could build basically anything it wanted, anywhere in New York, and whose authority to do so was protected by the United States Constitution. These bond covenants had turned Triborough Authority's powers into grant, but nothing in them guaranteed that Robert Moses would remain its chairman. So, how did Moses retain this position for 30 years? Part of the answer is, again, the law. Moses drafted the Triborough Act so that its commissioners could be removed mid-term only on, quote, stated charges after a formal hearing, end quote. But any differences he had with a mayor or governor were more about what he decided to build, and whom he displaced to build it, rather than his personal conduct. As Caro himself writes, quote, Robert Moses was not himself corrupt. He was, in fact, as uninterested in obtaining payoffs for himself as any public servant who ever lived. In the politicians' phrase, he was 'money honest,' end quote. So there were never any formal charges to bring. But this only explains why he wasn't fired in the middle of his term. Why was he reappointed every six years? Well, at his peak, Moses was a Hydra with a dozen heads. He held 12 different government posts at the same time: City Construction Coordinator, Chairman of the State Council of Parks, Chairman of the State Power Authority, and so on. Some of these positions were appointed by the Mayor, some of them by the Governor, and so no single person could cut off the heads simultaneously. And removing him from any one position meant that you were making an enemy with the man who would still be the most powerful person in New York. More importantly, no mayor or governor wanted to fire him. The way we remember Robert Moses today is based on a reputation that was calcified in the 1970s, when The Power Broker came out, and when New York was experiencing a dark age of crime, deindustrialization, and white flight. Moses got much of the blame for this, after all, the subtitle of The Power Broker is, Robert Moses and the Fall of New York. But in my opinion, this crisis had more to do with larger structural factors that had nothing to do with Moses himself. Cities that he had nothing to do with, like Detroit and St. Louis, experienced a much more dramatic collapse. For the vast majority of his career, Moses was incredibly popular. In the 1930s and 1940s, New York high schools voted Moses the most admired New Yorker. This was because he had delivered, over and over again, public works which millions of New Yorkers got to use and enjoy. The beaches, parks, pools. They used to dive off a dock into the murky River, but now it's splashing the looks in a grand, up-to-date leader. It cost a cool million and the kids say it's worth it. And he did so at a speed and a scale that nobody had seen before. In his first four months as the city's Park Commissioner, he kept crews working around the clock and finished 1,700 park renovations, and he did this all without a whiff of personal scandal. As Caro writes in The Power Broker, quote, It was not unusual at park and playground opening ceremonies for children, prodded by their parents, to break into the cheer, "2-4-6-8, who do we appreciate? Mr. Moses, Mr. Moses, Mr. Moses!" end quote. Importantly, Moses could deliver these enormous and popular public works within a time frame that fit into a single election cycle. If the mayor gave Robert Moses the power to build something, he could expect to be standing at its ribbon-cutting before he was up for reelection. Robert Moses, Mayor Wagner, and Cardinal Spellman join other dignitaries in a ribbon-cutting at the Brooklyn side of the bridge. Today it's almost unimaginable that the government could construct big things in a year or two. For example, construction on the Second Avenue subway in New York began in 1972, and the first section was open in 2017. And just these initial two miles have cost the taxpayers $4.5 billion. A lot of the funding for public works comes from Washington. Cities that receive the most money are the ones that have finalized their blueprints and can start breaking ground as soon as a new federal initiative is announced. Moses had the largest and best team of planners and engineers in the entire country. And because of that, by 1936, New York was getting 1/7 of the money that the federal government had set aside for Depression-era work relief for the entire nation. And the projects that this funding was going towards were the ones chosen by Moses. If the mayor had some different idea about what should be constructed, he had to draw up his own plans which he didn't have the independent technical expertise to do so. So he could either build the projects that Moses wanted to build, or he could build nothing at all. Between his ability to get things done, his popularity, and his control over money, Moses had a lot of leverage, and he was not shy about using it. Far from being worried about getting fired, Moses would constantly threaten to quit whenever he didn't get his way. Under Mayor Wagner, one aide said that, quote, it seemed like he was resigning about one thing or another about twice a day, end quote. He threatened resignation to Mayor La Guardia so often, and over such small things, that La Guardia's lawyer got a stack of forms printed that read, quote, "I, Robert Moses, do hereby resign as ____," end quote, to save Moses the trouble of writing it out every single time. But behind that joke was real fear. If a mayor accepted that resignation, not only would he be forfeiting the completion of whatever projects Moses was working on, along with the money that was apportioned for those projects, he'd also be picking a public fight with the most respected official in all of New York. Maybe this the most important thing. It's just sheer stubbornness. Moses had spent years befriending the owners of the city's newspapers. He spent many weekends at the summer home of the Sulzbergers, which is the family that owns The New York Times. The Mayor knew that if Moses resigned, the next morning, all the front pages would tell the same story about how a man above politics was driven out by a craven politician because he refused to compromise the city's interests. And the power that Moses derived from his popularity with the masses was nothing compared to the power that he derived from his connections with New York's powerful private interests. We are very used today to NIMBY, Not In My Backyard, where powerful but concentrated interest groups manage to stymie projects, despite their broad popularity. Moses built the concentrated faction for YIMBY, Yes In My Backyard. He built a graft machine that ensured that the most powerful people in the state would all benefit when new construction happened. The banks got to buy his bonds at a steep discount without that competitive bidding, the unions got more jobs, politically connected lawyers got legal fees, and party bosses got paid to broker insurance policies to bridges that had no plausible risk of falling down. Moses could then rely on these beneficiaries to call up anyone who stood in the way of one of his projects, whether that was a borough president or the governor himself. Caro paints Moses as somebody who began as an idealist, rather than somebody who craved power from the very start. In the 1910s, Moses was a brilliant young reformer who joined this new "Good Government" movement, which was trying to replace Tammany Hall's patronage with honest and competent administration. He spent years fighting this machine, but his reforms got killed. The machine won, and Moses learned that ideals are useless without the power to realize them. Caro writes, quote, In the beginning, and for decades of his career, the power Robert Moses amassed was the servant of his dreams, amassed for their sake, so that his gigantic, city-shaping visions could become reality. But power is not an instrument that its possessor can use with impunity. It is a drug that creates in the user a need for larger and larger doses, and Moses was a user, end quote. Lyndon Johnson's story, on the other hand, is kind of the opposite. Through most of his career, there's little evidence that he had a coherent political ideology. He was a ruthless power-seeker who embraced whatever happened to be the most expedient thing to do at that time. When Johnson first entered the U.S. Senate, he realized that the most powerful men there were the conservative Southern Dixiecrats, and the Johnson that they encountered was one who molded himself to their form. While writing Master of the Senate, volume 3 of his Johnson biographies, Caro interviewed former Georgia Senator Herman Talmadge. He asked Talmadge what impression he got from freshman Senator Lyndon Johnson about the proper relationship between white people and black people. And now quoting Caro's recounting of this exchange, quote, "Master and servant." So I, Caro, asked, "Well, didn't Johnson have any sympathy for blacks, any desire to improve their lot?" My notes say "long pause." And then I wrote Talmadge's two-word reply, "None indicated." Another pause, and then: "He was with us in his heart. I believed him. I believed him," end quote. Of course, we know that once he became president, Johnson spearheaded the 1964 Civil Rights Act and the 1965 Voting Rights Act, the most important civil rights laws since Reconstruction. So, Moses began as a naive idealist, but in Caro's telling, became somebody seeking power for its own sake. Johnson, on the other hand, was an opportunist from the very start. And it was only once he had real power that he used it for ideals few knew that he had. Before he was president, Lyndon Johnson was Vice President, and before he was Vice President, Lyndon Johnson was in the Senate. And Lyndon Johnson was the most powerful Senate Majority Leader, perhaps the most powerful legislator period, in the history of this country. No leader before Johnson had wielded this kind of power because the Senate had deliberately been set up by the founders to not be led. When Johnson asked the Library of Congress for a list of the Majority Leader's formal powers, he found that there was exactly one: the right to be recognized first on the floor. Before Johnson, the Majority Leader was less a leader and more a lightning rod. He'd be blamed in the national press and in his home state for everything that the Senate failed to do, yet he had no actual power to do anything about those failures. Being in this predicament was so politically toxic that Johnson's two predecessors as Majority Leaders had both lost their own Senate seats. A big part of the reason that Johnson became his party's leader at just 44 years old, in a Senate whose average age was around 60, was that hardly anybody else wanted this thankless and useless job. And in their hands, the job would have remained useless, but they were not Lyndon Johnson. From this position, Johnson turned himself into the second most powerful person in America, right after the president, and he used three different tactics to do so. First, he gained leverage. He made sure that he had things to promise, and things to threaten with. Johnson transformed the Senate's rules, institutions, and norms so that he alone had the power to grant important favors to his colleagues. If a senator needed a bill passed, Johnson was the one who could get it scheduled on the floor and persuade the committee chairman to report it out. And if the bill needed help in the House, Johnson was the one who had a direct line to Speaker Sam Rayburn. And if the senator needed money for his next campaign, so that he could make sure he remained a senator, Johnson was the one who could put in a good word with the oilmen from his native Texas. Johnson also put himself in a position to control committee assignments, which are the single biggest factor that shapes how a senator's whole career plays out. For about a century, they'd been handed out based on seniority. Practically overnight, Johnson changed the system so that many seats were no longer being settled by seniority, but rather negotiated by the party's Steering Committee, which Johnson controlled. If you wanted a good seat, you had to do what Johnson wanted. In 1957, Estes Kefauver voted against Johnson on an important motion. That very same week, the highly-coveted open seat on the Foreign Relations Committee, which Kefauver was expecting to get, was instead handed over to a far more junior senator named John F. Kennedy. The real political genius of Lyndon Johnson was identifying and then gaining control over every possible source of leverage over his colleagues. Whatever a senator needed to survive, whether that was money, committee seats, or the passage of bills that mattered to his home state, Johnson could give, and Johnson could withhold. The second important tactic was setting the agenda. What is said by a bill that passes depends on which version actually comes up for a vote, and with what final modifications. Whoever gets to set this agenda controls the outcome. So you won't be surprised by who put himself in this position. Johnson's main tool for controlling the agenda was the unanimous-consent agreement. By default, Senate debate is unlimited. A senator can talk about a bill for as long as he likes. And as he does so, he can also offer amendments that have nothing to do with that bill, each of which can stall the bill even further. A unanimous-consent agreement sets those freedoms aside for a single bill. It fixes the allotted time for debate and constrains the opportunity to amend it. Earlier leaders had rarely used such agreements, and even when they had, they'd only use them after weeks of debate on a bill that had already been exhausted in its deliberations. Johnson made their use the norm in the Senate. He wouldn't even allow a bill to reach the floor until such an agreement was in place. And the reason was that Johnson wanted to control which bills reached the floor, and to keep the floor from changing anybody's mind, because he wanted every vote settled beforehand through the wheeling and dealing where his leverage worked best. The last thing he wanted was for a long public debate about the merits of some bill to change a senator's mind at the very last minute, or for a surprise amendment to unravel a deal that he had spent weeks painstakingly brokering. As Senator Paul Douglas put it, under Johnson, all the actions take place off-stage before the play begins. And the third tactic that Johnson employed was brokering all the deals to his advantage. Johnson made sure that as much of the Senate's information as possible ran through him. He had every committee assignment staffer to tell him what was being drafted, his aides tracked what every senator was up to, and he spent his own days in the cloakroom and in the hallways cajoling and listening to his colleagues. So he knew exactly what each senator really wanted and what he would and wouldn't give up to get it. In any session, the Senate faces hundreds of possible bills and amendments. A senator's vote on one bill can be traded for another senator's vote on a different one. And any single bill can be amended a dozen different ways to win somebody over. So there's almost an endless combination of possible deals to be made. Johnson made sure that he was the one instigating and brokering most of these deals. As a result, he would be making sure that any deal left Johnson coming out ahead. A good example is how he got the Civil Rights Act of 1957 passed through the Senate. Johnson was desperate to make sure that at least some civil rights bill passed that year, even if it was weak. That's because he wanted to become president, but no Southerner with his record of consistently voting against civil rights could hope to win the Democratic presidential nomination in 1960. The problem was there seemed to be no bill strong enough to win the Northern Liberals, yet weak enough that the Southerners wouldn't filibuster it. The sticking point was who would preside over the court cases where Southern electoral officials were accused of suppressing black voters. Southern senators insisted on jury trials, but Northern senators were livid, because in their view, this would gut the whole bill. The entire point of the law was to combat the exclusion of black citizens from the voting rolls. But Southern states required jurors to be registered voters, and an all-white Southern jury would never convict a white registrar for turning away black voters. And so any version that one side could accept, the other would reject. A less creative legislator would have backed down. But over the course of seven months, Johnson built a masterful deal that brought together wholly unrelated factions, and whose byproduct included the passage of this civil rights law. Western senators got a vote on a federal dam that they had wanted for years, but in Idaho and Oregon, Midwestern Republicans who had powerful railroad unions in their state got to a right to a jury trial extended to striking workers, and Moderates got a clause guaranteeing that black citizens could serve on federal juries, so that they could tell voters that they had created a new civil right. Meanwhile, the jury trial guarantee for Southern election officials stayed in, which kept the bill weak enough that Southerners agreed not to filibuster it. A dam in Idaho and protection to union workers and federal jury rules had nothing to do with voting rights in the South. At least, not to anyone who lacked the inside knowledge and political creativity of Lyndon Johnson. Caro's books are the best-written accounts of how political power actually works, but they are written so compellingly, and with characters more vivid and stories more gripping than any novel, that it's very easy to get so engrossed that you just keep turning the pages, and you never stop and ask yourself, what are the lessons these stories are teaching about power? I wanted to make this video essay both so that I could think through what these lessons actually are, but also so that you would have the context necessary to watch my interview with Caro and get as much out of it as I did. You'll be able to watch the interview tomorrow. So, by the time you're listening to this, it might already be out. I hope you enjoy it.

How to Build the Next SpaceX or Tesla

a16z(305.0K)·49:17·6.8K views·2026-10-29
Erin Price-WrightInvestor, a16z Connor LoveInvestor, a16z Elena BurgerInvestor, a16z
TL;DRThe rebuilding of America's industrial base is uniting defense, energy, manufacturing, and robotics under a single wave of American Dynamism, driven by private capital funding physical infrastructure in advance of government demand.
Consequences of Hollowing Out: Decades of hollowing out the industrial base have created real battlefield consequences for American soldiers and allies, proving that a defense industrial base cannot exist without a robust underlying industrial base. - The Convergence of Forces:...

TL;DR

The rebuilding of America's industrial base is uniting defense, energy, manufacturing, and robotics under a single wave of American Dynamism, driven by private capital funding physical infrastructure in advance of government demand.

Rebuilding the Industrial Base & The Dual-Use Reality

  • Consequences of Hollowing Out: Decades of hollowing out the industrial base have created real battlefield consequences for American soldiers and allies, proving that a defense industrial base cannot exist without a robust underlying industrial base.
  • The Convergence of Forces: A combination of geopolitical conflict, evolving capital markets, and an entrepreneurial base willing to build in hard sectors is driving the current industrial renaissance.
  • End-to-End Lens: The AD50 list evaluates American Dynamism companies across three distinct pillars: inputs (materials), factories, and end systems.
  • The Dual-Use Model: While federal demand often acts as the tip of the spear and subsidizes early development costs, these factories and supply chains ultimately serve massive commercial and consumer markets (e.g., Waymo, consumer electronics).
  • The DJI Precedent: DJI started in consumer drones, expanded into national defense, and transformed into the world's largest producer of robot actuators (the hardest part of building robots), proving how military process knowledge transfers to physical AI.

Software, AI, and Manufacturing Innovation

  • AI in the Physical World: While AI is a powerful tool for planning, optimization, and reprogramming, speakers emphasize that "AI cannot turn a wrench yet."
  • Low-Mix, High-Volume vs. High-Mix, Low-Volume:
    • Historically, automation required low-mix, high-volume production.
    • Software tools (such as Claude) now empower manufacturing technicians to reconfigure low-volume, high-mix lines in 20 minutes instead of waiting 3 months for a system integrator and spending hundreds of thousands of dollars.
  • Vertical Integration & Design for Manufacturing: Following the playbook of SpaceX and Tesla, modern founders are vertically integrating where supplier bases are weak and designing for manufacturing from the outset to achieve high-rate production (e.g., facilities built by Anduril, Seronic, Hadrian).
  • Supply Chain Quality Rebound:
    • Three years ago, startups threw away 50% of incoming PCB boards due to quality failures.
    • New software-native PCB suppliers in the US now achieve 70% to 90% passing rates.

Capital Markets, Private Risk, and the Defense "Valley of Death"

  • Private Capital Pre-Funding: Unlike historical models where companies waited for government contracts, private venture capital, private equity, and major credit firms (like JPMorgan committing trillions to the physical world) are funding physical infrastructure and production *before* government demand is guaranteed.
  • Bypassing the Valley of Death: Risk capital is helping startups bridge the historic defense "valley of death" by financing capacity ahead of formal requirements.
  • The Capital Advantage: Unlike China's top-down directed industrial policy, America's bottom-up capital markets follow technological and entrepreneurial success, offering greater flexibility and adaptability.
  • The New Economic Scale: Physical industries like mining, defense, and energy are no longer just matters of national security—they are now capable of becoming some of the most valuable companies in the world, attracting top-tier engineering talent.

Co-Engineering and the "Manufacturing Middle"

  • The Legacy Bottleneck: Traditional OEMs (like Boeing calling Honeywell for a 737 landing gear) rely on 50-year-old subsystems because legacy suppliers are in "receive mode" and lack incentives to innovate.
  • Rise of the Manufacturing Middle: A new wave of middle-layer supply companies is emerging to co-engineer rethought subcomponents (actuators, switches, valves, power electronics) designed for modern materials and software-native architectures.
  • Grid and Electronics Transformation: The energy grid is undergoing a step change from static, 1970s mechanical systems to modern, software-native power electronics and battery storage, enabling a complete redesign of the underlying supply chain.

The Brutal Reality of Physical-World Building

  • Missionary Founders: Building physical companies requires traveling to remote, off-grid locations, managing tough negotiations, and enduring weeks away from family—undertaken only by deeply passionate, missionary founders.
  • Tangible Community Impact: Unlike abstract software companies siloed in coastal cities, data centers, power plants, and mines are built in Middle America; long-term success requires bringing local communities along for the economic upside.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Defense Risk Capital Deployment: Risk dollars entering the defense ecosystem in the first half of 2026 will surpass all of last year combined, which was already multiples of previous years — Connor Love

Notable Quotes

"The biggest change is, those companies weren't just the most important thing to us, now they can be the most valuable companies. And that's like a big distinction that that has happened." — Connor Love
"I don't think that that is a technology problem. I think that that's a... we need to do some soul searching about our will as an American people, and do we want conforming projects to be built, do we want to grow?" — Erin Price-Wright

And so what happens is companies—back then we didn't really have neoprimes or AD companies to serve, but what you had is you had primes who would just sit there and listen: tell me what you want, I'll develop the thing after you give me the money. And the outcome of that is the soldier gets a better product, gets something that actually fundamentally changes the battlefield and helps with deterrence or security or safety. And then more importantly, you get a time frame of a type of system that is actually built aligned with the requirement of the market and what technology can do today. I think if you see that again and again in defense, you're just going to get better products and fundamentally you'll have the neoprimes and the new companies start to take a lot of that market share. Like it has to be worth it. You're building a physical thing, you're not sitting in a desk on your computer, you're out in the world. It sounds kind of sexy and romantic, but when you're actually doing it, it's rough. The travel is really hard, the people you're engaging with, you're not calling up your suppliers, these are not like other startups who operate in the same kind of time scale and culture as you. These are really tough people to negotiate with and work with who have a very different culture, and if you're going to be putting yourself through that, it better be damn worth it. And so I think people really gravitate towards these problems that feel huge and enormous because these are real missionary founders that we see building in the space. Even as it's kind of heated up and there's more capital and it's gotten cool, it's still brutal. I think the biggest thing that has changed in American dynamism land in the last few years is we always knew that the companies being built in this category were really important to the fabric of America. I mean, again, when we talk about defense systems, when we talk about energy, when we talk about building manufacturing widgets that go into everything, that stuff's really important. It affects our safety, it affects how we live our life. But the biggest change is, those companies weren't just the most important thing to us, now they can be the most valuable companies. And that's a big distinction that has happened, which is it's not just solving a problem for safety or security, it's solving a problem for safety and security, and oh by the way, those can be some of the largest companies that exist in the world. And that's what technology has changed in these industries. I mean, mining has always been a big industry, defense has always been, energy, but it hasn't been economically worth it for a young smart person who has a lot of ambition to go after these categories. But I think companies like Palantir, Anduril, SpaceX have proved that you can build a really big business operating in these categories. And if you look at the top 10 most valuable companies in the world right now, the majority of them are hardware companies, they're building a physical product. We've kind of come full circle, and so you get the most ambitious founders of our generation who want to run towards that hard problem and know it's going to be worth it. And the beautiful thing about many of these industries, like mining or Rio Tinto has been around for a long time, Boeing's been around for a long time, good companies that are building good products, but I think when you take a first principles technology approach about what the audacious thing that needs to be built is, and then you get entrepreneurs like these folks that are on this 8050 list, like, yeah, I'm going to rebuild the American energy grid, that sounds fun. And that didn't exist 50 years ago, that didn't exist five years ago, it is something that is fundamentally America's best and brightest are building towards this category for the first time. And importantly, like Erin said, the opportunity is there. You see these examples of these companies that have redefined space launch, that have redefined military systems, that have redefined all these categories of American dynamism that we care about. And yeah, I think it's exciting. As someone who served in the military, you always think of what you do as something greater than yourself. I mean look, the capital markets exist to make money, we're not a charity, this is a capitalist society, but in the end, a lot of the companies on this 8050 list are doing something that is both going to be large and valuable, but also going to be really inherently important to the fabric of America. And that's pretty exciting. And you see that again and again and again, across not just our venture capital firms, but private equity now is starting to play in this category and big credit firms. Big credit firms. And that's so important, because the reality is in many of these companies in the AD world, they require a lot of capital. And we talked about that earlier, the concept of building in advance of the requirement they need. Maybe we're a little bit late on the energy side, maybe we're a little bit late on some of the defense systems, but look, the capital markets have woken up and we're putting hundreds of billions of dollars, not trillions of dollars, towards this category for the next 10 plus years. Yeah. Inside Exelon CEO Calvin Butler’s $41 Billion Bet And The Impact on The U.S. Electricity Grid

Forbes(2.2M)·54:33·1.6K views·2026-10-29
Calvin ButlerPresident and CEO, Exelon
TL;DRExelon President and CEO Calvin Butler discusses how the intersection of soaring electricity demand from AI data centers, manufacturing onshoring, and electrification is straining the U.S. power grid, while outlining his long-term capital strategy and community engagement initiatives.
Company Valuation and Capital Deployment: Capital Investment: Exelon is executing a $41.7 billion capital investment plan (up from $38 billion previously), representing a 10% growth over the last four years. 2026 Spending: The company plans to invest $10 billion in...

TL;DR: Exelon President and CEO Calvin Butler discusses how the intersection of soaring electricity demand from AI data centers, manufacturing onshoring, and electrification is straining the U.S. power grid, while outlining his long-term capital strategy and community engagement initiatives.

Business Scale, Capital Strategy, and Financial Performance

  • Company Valuation and Capital Deployment:
      • Capital Investment: Exelon is executing a $41.7 billion capital investment plan (up from $38 billion previously), representing a 10% growth over the last four years.
      • 2026 Spending: The company plans to invest $10 billion in infrastructure this year.
      • Market Position: Exelon trades on the Nasdaq under ticker EXC with a $41 billion market cap and serves major markets in Chicago, Pennsylvania, Delaware, the District of Columbia, and Maryland.
  • Pure Transmission and Distribution (T&D) Model:
      • Regulatory Structure: Exelon is a pure regulated T&D utility holding company and does not own generation assets.
      • Financial Impact: Because it does not own generation, Exelon has not experienced the direct financial boom from data centers that pure generation-owning utilities enjoy.
  • Cost Reduction and Investor Base:
      • Efficiency Targets: Butler has committed to eliminating $350 million in costs in 2027, part of a broader three-year initiative removing $1 billion in operational costs to address customer affordability.
      • Shareholder Metrics: International investors hold 28% of Exelon's portfolio, reflecting the company's long-term value strategy.

Grid Demand, Data Centers, and Reliability

  • Peak Demand Surges:
      • ComEd Growth: ComEd's peak demand reached 23 gigawatts in 2024 and is projected to exceed 30 gigawatts by the end of 2030.
      • Data Center Scale: A single large data center consumes 1 gigawatt of power, equivalent to the output of an entire nuclear reactor.
  • Reliability Rankings:
      • Exelon operates four utilities ranked among the most reliable in the U.S.: ComEd is #1, Pepco Holdings (DC, Delmarva, Ace) is #2, BGE (Baltimore) is #4, and PECO (Philadelphia) is #7.
  • Rate Cases and Stakeholder Engagement:
      • PECO Rate Case Withdrawal: Following criticism from Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro regarding messaging and lack of pre-work, Exelon pulled its recent rate case to improve upfront stakeholder communication.

Workforce, Supply Chain Diversity, and Leadership

  • Diversity and Inclusion Spend:
      • Supplier Diversity: Exelon reported $8 billion in historical spend with diverse suppliers, including minority-, women-, veteran-, and LGBTQ-certified businesses, maintaining this commitment without federal mandates because "it is good for business."
  • Workforce Evolution:
      • Modern Energy Jobs: Field operations have shifted from climbing poles to utilizing iPads for grid health assessments and piloting drones for line inspections.
  • Leadership Philosophy:
      • Cost Decisions: Butler acknowledges the difficulty of workforce reductions and cost-cutting measures, noting that leaders must make timely decisions to protect the enterprise's long-term health.
      • Management Mentors: Butler credits his late mother (a federal government IT worker) for his people skills and his late father (who worked at the Kincaid Power Station) for instilling a relentless work ethic.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Peak Demand Projections: Calvin Butler predicts that ComEd's peak electricity demand will exceed 30 gigawatts by the end of 2030.
  • Cost Reduction Commitments: Calvin Butler commits to eliminating $350 million in operational costs in 2027.
  • Grid Reliability Warnings: Calvin Butler warns that if the utility sector continues operating under "status quo" regulatory frameworks without adapting to modern demand growth, U.S. cities risk facing blackouts.

Notable Quotes

"If you have great demand, which the data centers are part of that equation, and inadequate supply, you’re going to have high prices... No one cares how reliable you are if they can’t afford to pay the bill." — Calvin Butler
"It’s like blaming your Uber driver for the price of food. You know, he’s like, 'Hey, I’m delivering it to you.'... But goes back to no one cares. At the end of the day, people are saying, look, this is essential for my quality of life and the bill’s too high." — Calvin Butler
"We are in a new time frame. We're in a new game and you can't play by the old rules and expect a different result... The grid is letting you know, the warning lights are flashing, but we're not talking about it." — Calvin Butler

Taking on ServiceNow: Coding Agents for the Rest of Us with Serval Co-Founder and CEO Jake Stauch

No Priors: AI, Machine Learning, Tech, & Startups(99.4K)·38:06·1.3K views·2026-10-29
Sarah GuoHost, No Priors Elad GilHost, No Priors Jake StauchCo-founder and CEO, Serval
TL;DRServal is a 2.5-year-old enterprise AI platform providing coding agents and guardrails for IT departments to automate internal workflows, directly challenging legacy service management systems like ServiceNow.
Co-founder and CEO Jake Stauch explains that by combining AI-generated code with strict enterprise controls—such as deterministic approvals, staging environments, and audit trails—Serval enables non-engineers across the enterprise to safely build internal software. Core Product...

TL;DR

Serval is a 2.5-year-old enterprise AI platform providing coding agents and guardrails for IT departments to automate internal workflows, directly challenging legacy service management systems like ServiceNow. Co-founder and CEO Jake Stauch explains that by combining AI-generated code with strict enterprise controls—such as deterministic approvals, staging environments, and audit trails—Serval enables non-engineers across the enterprise to safely build internal software.

Company Background & Founding Vision

  • Core Product & Mission: Serval offers enterprise automation and service management through codegen agents, functioning as an AI platform for IT departments to build internal products for HR, finance, legal, and people teams.
  • Origin Story:
      • CEO Jake Stauch discovered the pain point while working at Verkada (enterprise physical security products), where IT teams repeatedly named ServiceNow as their most expensive and hated expense.
      • Started 2.5 years ago by Jake Stauch and co-founder Alex in a living room with the deliberate ambition to build the largest enterprise software company of all time and directly replace ServiceNow.
  • Headcount Growth: Scaled from 20 employees in January to 200 people today, with hiring velocity expected to increase further.

Product Architecture & Enterprise Guardrails

  • Code as Automation: Serval's core insight is that code is the best way to express automation, and AI makes it possible for non-technical users to leverage code rather than painful drag-and-drop workflow builders.
  • The Boundary and Guardrails: Serval acts as the security layer and ticketing system, allowing enterprises to grant elevated permissions through strict deterministic approval cycles, staging/approval gates, and full audit trails.
  • Catalyst Agent: An internal management agent built on top of Serval that helps admins and IT pros build underlying automations, configure approval rules, permission checks, and build policies through natural language.
  • Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC): Unlike consumer agents or unconstrained enterprise AI tools, Serval enforces a traditional engineering SDLC model with dev, staging, and production environments, user acceptance testing, and review gates.

Go-To-Market & Implementation Strategy

  • Deployment Team: Serval deploys both a "deployment strategist" and a "deployed software engineer" on-site with customers to map workflows, implement solutions, and manage migration.
  • Pro-Serval: An ongoing automation engineering service team that embeds with customers after initial deployment to support continuous automation demands.
  • Targeting the C-Suite: Serval sells top-down to IT leadership and C-levels, pitching the platform as a career-defining move for IT leaders (e.g., Fox Corporation adopted Serval company-wide, and their lead migration engineer won MVP of the entire company).
  • Decentralized Product Development: Serval has no dedicated product function; instead, engineers are decentralized and immersed in customer Slack channels (with most engineers on at least one customer call per day) to build features iteratively based on real-time feedback.

Industry Perspectives & The SaaS Landscape

  • Personal vs. Enterprise Agents: While consumer "personal agents" excel in SMB and mid-market settings, large enterprises require strict permissions and centralized IT control; Stauch predicts enterprise and personal work agents will remain distinct (analogous to corporate vs. personal email).
  • Token Spend and ROI: While CIOs experienced an initial "token maxing" era followed by pullbacks to measure ROI, long-term spend is projected to rise; Serval prices on workflows run rather than raw tokens to better reflect actual work completed.
  • The "SaaS-pocalypse": Stauch believes core systems of record (ERP, HRIS, ITSM, CRM) will not disappear because enterprise software maintenance is complex and expensive, but hundreds of smaller point-solution SaaS applications will consolidate into core platforms.

Notable Quotes

"Serval is the boundary. Serval is the guardrails. We are the way that a large enterprise with a lot of rules and approvals and process and politics can adopt these tools safely and comfortably at scale." — Jake Stauch
"Everything has gone exactly according to our very delusional plans. We set out from the beginning to be the largest enterprise software company of all time. We actually made a list of all the companies, we circled ServiceNow and we said we're going to beat them and we're going to build something much bigger." — Jake Stauch
"IT actually wants to build stuff, and for most of the world, they don't have an engineering department, they have an IT department... We're building essentially the AI platform for them." — Jake Stauch

WHOOP CEO, Will Ahmed: 50B Hours of Health Data, a 4 Million Member Data Moat, & the FDA Fight

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·55:39·1.1K views·2026-10-29
Will AhmedCEO, WHOOP
TL;DRWHOOP CEO Will Ahmed discusses scaling the fitness wearable company to 4 million members, transitioning to a profitable subscription model, and leveraging over 50 billion hours of continuous physiological data to build AI health coaching tools.
Member Growth: Will Ahmed noted the company took about 12 years to reach 1 million members, another 18 months to hit 2 million, roughly 6 months to 3 million, and expects to end the year around 4 million members. Organization Size: Will Ahmed shared that WHOOP now employs...

TL;DR

WHOOP CEO Will Ahmed discusses scaling the fitness wearable company to 4 million members, transitioning to a profitable subscription model, and leveraging over 50 billion hours of continuous physiological data to build AI health coaching tools.

Company Footprint & Business Model

  • Member Growth: Will Ahmed noted the company took about 12 years to reach 1 million members, another 18 months to hit 2 million, roughly 6 months to 3 million, and expects to end the year around 4 million members.
  • Organization Size: Will Ahmed shared that WHOOP now employs 1,200 people.
  • The Subscription Pivot: Will Ahmed explained that in 2017 WHOOP sold consumers a one-time $500 hardware unit, but observed a "sales problem" despite high engagement. In 2018–2019, they shifted to a recurring subscription model ($15 to $30 a month, or roughly a dollar a day) which eliminated device replacement anxiety and changed the company DNA to obsess over user retention, engagement, and long-term hardware-agnostic software improvements.

Physiological Data, Metrics, & Health Insights

  • The 3 Core Pillars: Will Ahmed outlined WHOOP's primary metrics: Sleep (score, consistency, restorative slow-wave and REM sleep), Recovery (red, yellow, green dial from 0 to 100%), and Strain.
  • Data Scale: Will Ahmed highlighted that members wear WHOOP an average of 23.6 hours a day, accumulating just over 50 billion hours of continuous physiological data.
  • Heart Rate Variability (HRV): Will Ahmed explained HRV as the time between successive heartbeats—where higher variability indicates a healthier autonomic nervous system—which serves as a key longevity and stress indicator that naturally drops with age.
  • Acute Health Detection: Jason Calacanis and Will Ahmed discussed how baseline monitoring catches anomalous metrics (elevated respiratory rate, skin temperature, and plummeting HRV) days ahead of acute events like COVID-19, lung inflammation, and cancers (Hodgkin lymphoma and testicular cancer).
  • The WHOOP Journal: Will Ahmed noted that over 50% of members journal daily, tracking 100+ lifestyle behaviors (alcohol, hydration, travel, nutrition) that generate personalized behavioral insights showing how specific habits (e.g., warm baths, late meals, alcohol) positively or negatively impact recovery scores.

AI Integration & New Features

  • AI Coach: Will Ahmed reported that about 80% of members interact with the AI assistant, which utilizes internal foundational models trained on WHOOP's physiological data, advanced labs/blood work, medical records, and member journals.
  • Targeted Health Features: Will Ahmed detailed newly released capabilities, including ECG monitoring, AFib detection, advanced blood labs, WHOOP Age (health span), and blood pressure insights.
  • GLP-1 Tracking: Will Ahmed and Jason Calacanis discussed WHOOP's dedicated GLP-1 tracking program, designed to combat muscle loss (which accounts for ~50% of weight lost on GLP-1s) by prompting users toward increased protein consumption, hydration, step goals, and strength training.

Regulatory Landscape & The FDA

  • Blood Pressure Controversy: Will Ahmed addressed WHOOP's clash with the FDA over its unapproved wellness blood pressure feature, noting that despite an initial warning letter and public dispute, WHOOP stood its ground, contributing to updated FDA guidelines recognizing blood pressure wellness applications.
  • FDA Approvals: Will Ahmed noted that while WHOOP utilizes wellness classifications for rapid innovation, it also pursues formal medical clearances for features like its 510(k) FDA-cleared ECG wrist screening.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Will Ahmed predicted that WHOOP will end the current year with 4 million members.

Notable Quotes

*"When you're a subscription business, you become really obsessed with your existing members, arguably more so even than the new members. And so it's all about innovation cycles and continuing to rapidly improve the product and obsess over the member experience."* — Will Ahmed

So HRV stands for heart rate variability, it's the amount of time between successive beats of the heart. And and it's a little counterintuitive, but the more variability in time between successive beats of the heart, the healthier your overall system is operating. And it's this lens into your autonomic nervous system, which consists of sympathetic and parasympathetic activity. So sympathetic activation, heart rate up, blood pressure up, respiration up. it's what happens, you know, when you have a thought like that there's a lion in the room, or, you know, when you start to exercise or you're thinking about something stressful. conversely, parasympathetic's all the opposite. Heart rate down, blood pressure down, respiration down. it's what helps you fall asleep. If you, exhale for a long period of time, that's parasympathetic. And what you want is for for sympathetic activity and parasympathetic activity to be coupled, and so your body's kind of constantly regulating its environment. And when that's the case, it, it means you have a higher heart rate variability. That the measurement will be higher. And we're measuring heart rate variability 24/7. It was really one of the the key innovations in founding the company. I did all this medical research when I was a student at Harvard, and heart rate variability was the thing that like jumped off the page to me because there was really interesting research on it, but it couldn't be measured 24/7. And and so that's where, you know, part of where WHOOP was born out of. Anyway, heart rate variability is something that we use for stress monitoring, it's something we use for our recovery score, and, and we baseline everyone on WHOOP, and then we're able to show you every day, are you within your baseline, are you a little higher, you're a little lower. And when you look at trends of this over years, you start to get a sense for, is someone getting healthier over time or or less healthy? It's a really important longevity indicator. typically speaking, as you get older, your heart rate variability will drop. Well, just to say, like it's been a certainly a long process for Whoop and working with the FDA, and we've worked with them in a lot of different contexts, which I'll talk about here in a second. And, you know, the history of very innovative technology with, you know, somewhat dated regulatory policies is a history that has controversy from time to time. So I'm not surprised that we've had some challenges or disagreements along the way. But I'm actually really happy with with our relationship with the FDA today. Now, four years ago, I really wanted to be able to do blood pressure monitoring. And historically, you needed a cuff for that and it was really a pretty difficult signal processing problem. There was no product on the market that could measure blood pressure accurately from the wrist. And so we set out to see if we could do it. And one of the ways that we collected a huge data set on it was in collaboration with our members where we asked members if they wanted to participate in a stress, sleep and blood pressure study. And as part of that we actually had 70,000 people enroll. So think about, you know, the ability to generate studies at at a huge scale fast. and and so people were actually uploading their blood pressure readings every single day for a number of weeks. And what that allowed us to do was to build out a pretty powerful data set on on blood pressure monitoring and also to be able to train that alongside all of the physiological data that we were already collecting. Anyway, fast forward, we get to a period where all of a sudden we've got a very powerful algorithm for measuring blood pressure. And that's one of the things that we do, I think very well at Whoop is, you know, we pick a hard physiological signal and then we say we want to be able to measure it as accurately or within a similar accuracy as a medical device. and then we look to bring that to consumers. And there was controversy around blood pressure monitoring before we launched because prior to launching it, historically blood pressure had been under the category of physiological signals that needed to be a quote-unquote medical device. And just as some, you know, background for for our listeners, the FDA is really supposed to evaluate products that have a medical diagnosis and and then if you're not providing a medical diagnosis, then it falls under a category called wellness. And and there's like a pretty good, you know, history for this with with heart rate monitoring for example, where if you do heart rate monitoring for exercise, that's wellness. If you do heart rate monitoring for sleep, that's wellness. If you do heart rate monitoring for atrial fibrillation detection or heart attack prediction, Okay, that needs to be regulated. So the FDA in the past had a pretty clear distinction for heart rate. This is wellness, this is medical, right? With blood pressure, they said that if you're measuring blood pressure, it has to be medical. But the problem with that is there's an avalanche of research that supports that not surprisingly, blood pressure affects your stress, it affects your sleep, it affects your overall well-being. And we felt like there was a pretty clear lane for us to introduce a blood pressure feature that was a wellness feature. And so we launched the feature, the FDA you know, came back and said that they felt like it needed to be regulated. They actually sent us a warning letter. There was a big disagreement that played out that was quite public about it. but we, you know, we stuck to our guns and and I ultimately really believe in in the way that we approached it. And through a long process, the FDA actually has since updated their guidelines, I think based on our case, but also based on feedback from the industry, that supports that blood pressure can also have wellness applications. And and so, you know, our blood pressure insights feature is still available for consumers. And just to say it, we have we have these conversations with the FDA across a lot of different categories. Whoop also has medical clearances from the FDA. So, our ECG feature where you can literally do an ECG from your wrist by just putting your fingers on the Whoop, that's an FDA cleared product, 510K. And so, in some cases, we're we're going through a process of clinical trials to get FDA approval, in other cases, we want to bring a product to market faster. and we also believe it's a wellness application.
